Answering that inside the deliverable itself turns a weakness into a governance feature: business sign-off becomes row-by-row ("confirm / soften / remove") rather than a whole-deliverable debate, and the closing-loop email writes itself from the "confirm" rows. **How to apply:** For every MOM, closing-loop report, gap-analysis, quarterly review, or multi-document synthesis that layers analysis on sources — add the Assumptions Register tab *before* sending. Target ~10–15 hard assumptions and 3–5 soft ones as a healthy range; fewer usually signals shallow analysis, more signals scope creep. Use severity pills (High for classifications and invented actions, Medium for framing choices, Low for time derivations and colour conventions). Cross-link from every analytical claim in the body back to its assumption row via an inline `→ Assumption #N` chip (lesson #434).

434. **Use prefix-based anchor IDs (#tab-item) + a JS hash resolver so cross-tab deep-links Just Work in multi-tab HTMLs** — On 2026-04-20, v3 of the BMS MOM HTML added cross-tab navigation after v2 had grown to six tabs and reviewers couldn't follow the claim-chain MOM Decision #1 → Divergence #1 → Email gap #9 → Consolidated row 27 → Assumption #14. Each pivotal element got an ID of the form `<tab-prefix>-<item>` (e.g. `study-div-2`, `email-gap-9`, `mom-dec-1`, `act-27`, `ass-hard-1`). A tiny JS map `{exec:'exec', mom:'mom', email:'email', study:'study', act:'actions', ass:'assumptions'}` plus a `resolveHash()` function parses `location.hash`, extracts the prefix, activates the matching tab via the existing tab-switcher, then `scrollIntoView` with a 2-second yellow-flash animation on the target. A delegated `document.addEventListener('click', ...)` intercepts all internal `<a href="#...">` clicks so the flow is one click from anywhere. Each tab also got a "Top-Picked Cross-References" callout at the top — 5–6 **curated** jumps (labelled by intent: "Drill into finding 1 →", "Divergence #2 ↔ source →", "Challenge my analysis →"), not auto-generated from every anchor. **Why:** In a long multi-tab report, the value isn't tab independence — it's the web of relationships between tabs. Without explicit cross-links, readers have to search the other tabs by hand, lose context, and give up on verifying individual claims. Editorial curation matters more than completeness: 5–6 hand-picked jumps per tab are more useful than 50 auto-generated ones because they signal "this is what a reviewer should trace first." **How to apply:** Apply to any HTML with ≥ 4 tabs or where the same logical entity appears in multiple places (OKR dashboard KRs, Outlook report categories, Weekly Meeting agenda items, gap-analyses, multi-doc syntheses). Prefix conventions: use the tab's short name (max 5 chars) as prefix so hashes stay readable (`#mom-act-3` not `#meeting-minutes-action-item-3`). Add `scroll-margin-top: 180px` to the `.xref-target` CSS class so the sticky header doesn't cover the flash animation. Skip for single-tab pages or purely linear reports — the cost isn't worth it.

435. **Always cross-check a MOM's "accepted limitations" against the EM's follow-up email — technical dispositions often close more gaps than the MOM records** — On 2026-04-20, the Apr-15 Aqraboon ↔ BMS integration MOM recorded 10 sync gaps between the two systems as "accepted current state — not defects" (Decision #1, "sync boundaries are acknowledged and accepted"). The MOM's out-of-scope list then reinforced the passive framing by listing "real-time reverse synchronization from BMS to Aqraboon" as out of scope. But Mohamed Salah Basha's follow-up email at 16:24 Doha (≈3 hours after the meeting) contained an "Initial Investigation" column against each of the 10 gaps with dispositions like "Current QC APIs should cover this" (gap 1), "Not a valid business case — prevent in AQ" (gap 2), "New QC API once Wallet finished return implementation" (gap 3), "Balance-update flag fix" (gap 7), "Lock AQ beneficiaries in BMS" (gap 9). Bucketing the 10 dispositions yielded: 5 **Closeable now** (gaps 1, 6, 7, 8, 10), 2 **Scope out** (gaps 2, 5), 1 **Pending dependency** (gap 3 blocked by Wallet team's return API), 2 **Genuinely open** (gaps 4, 9). The MOM's passive language had under-reported the team's actual technical position by 7 gaps — copying it into the closing-loop email to Semir/Bahaa would have weakened the governance artifact. **Why:** An MOM is a meeting-time record; it captures what was agreed by consensus, which tends toward conservative "accept current state" language when the technical implications aren't fully unpacked in the room. The EM's written follow-up arrives with 2–4 hours of additional reflection and technical analysis. Treating both documents as equal-status inputs to the closing-loop artefact — not treating the MOM as the only authoritative source — is what produces a closing-loop that actually reflects the team's position. **How to apply:** For every multi-system integration MOM (BMS / Wallet / Aqraboon / HUB / and similar), wait for the post-meeting email (up to 24 hours) before drafting the closing-loop. Render the email verbatim in a dedicated tab of the deliverable (next to the MOM verbatim). In the Deep Study tab add a "Reclassification" table using the five-bucket taxonomy (**Closeable now / Close with policy / Scope out / Pending dependency / Genuinely open**) and a headline count (e.g. "5 / 2 / 1 / 2"). Flag the reclassification as a PM interpretation in the Assumptions Register (per lesson #433) so stakeholders can challenge individual bucket assignments without rejecting the framework. The closing-loop email then goes out *from the reclassified list*, not from the MOM's passive language.

## Bilingual Dashboard Completeness & Roster Modeling (Apr 20, 2026)

436. **Search local project data files BEFORE saying "not in the knowledge base"** — On 2026-04-20, when asked about team member "Mostaf Labib" for the Team Velocity tab's roster, I ran ragtools `search_knowledge_base` against every Department_Workflows_and_Roles, OKR_KPI_Dashboard, and RELIEF_CENTER_PROJECT_STATUS doc — came back empty — and told the user "not found anywhere". The user pushed back with "search deep in your memory". A plain Grep for `Labib` across `D:\Project Management\` turned up `devops_cache.json` in the exact same folder as the dashboard, with `"assignee": "Mostaf Labib"` on 2 tasks in KhairGate (BMS) Sprint 3 — the authoritative source the dashboard itself reads from. Same pass surfaced 2 more unmapped assignees (Afthab P A, Badr Mohamed Sayed Mohamed Badr) the KB didn't have either. **Why:** Ragtools indexes *documentation* (what PMs write about the team). But the authoritative source of who exists in the system is the application's own cache/data files (`devops_cache.json`, exported CSV rosters, Azure DevOps user cache). Documentation lags reality by weeks-to-months; cache files refresh daily. Saying "not in KB" when the user asked "is this person real" is a retrieval failure dressed up as an answer. **How to apply:** When the question is "does X exist" (person, project name, field value, feature flag) rather than "what do we know about X" (narrative, context, history), search in this order: (1) the current project's own data files (JSON/CSV/MD in the same folder as the artefact you're building or editing); (2) ragtools for context about what was found in step 1; (3) conversation memory. Never skip step 1. Local data is authoritative; the indexed KB is a derivative and always stale by definition.

437. **Never embed HTML tags inside `data-i18n` translation values — split into separate spans instead** — On 2026-04-20, the Team Velocity tab's "How this tab works" footer had three notes shaped `<div data-i18n="tv-note-1"><strong>Velocity = ...</strong> ClosedDate is stamped ...</div>`, with matching Arabic values in the T map containing literal `<strong>...</strong>` tags. When the user toggled AR, the `<strong>` rendered as visible text "<strong>" on-screen because `applyLang()` uses `el.textContent = T[key]`, which stringifies tags literally. Worse, subsequent toggle back to EN also lost the bold because `data-i18n-original` had captured the plain-text form during the first AR pass. Fix was to restructure each note into two adjacent spans — `<span data-i18n="tv-note-1-lead" style="font-weight:700;">…</span> <span data-i18n="tv-note-1-tail">…</span>` — with plain-text values in the T map. **Why:** Dashboard i18n systems built around `textContent` are incompatible with HTML markup inside translation strings. The contract is: one `data-i18n` element = one contiguous piece of text. Styling (bold, italic, colour) must live on the element itself via CSS or inline style, not inside the translatable string. If you need mid-string emphasis, split the string into multiple sibling elements each with its own `data-i18n` key. **How to apply:** (1) When writing new bilingual HTML, never put `<strong>`, `<em>`, `<b>`, `<i>`, `<code>`, `<a>`, or any other tag inside a `data-i18n` element's direct text content if the i18n system uses `textContent`. (2) When auditing an existing bilingual page, Grep for `data-i18n="[^"]*"><[a-z]` to find offending cells. (3) If you *must* preserve inline tags (legacy content), switch that one element to `innerHTML` replacement in the applyLang walker — but document the exception clearly because it opens an XSS vector if any T value is untrusted.

438. **Store timestamps as ISO in `localStorage`; format with the current locale at render time** — On 2026-04-20, the Project Management Handling dashboard stored the last-fetch timestamp in `FETCH_TS_KEY` as a pre-formatted string `"4/19/2026, 3:19:52 PM"` (output of `toLocaleDateString() + ' ' + toLocaleTimeString()`). Toggling AR after a fetch could not flip the `"PM"` suffix to Arabic `م` because the stored value had already baked in the English formatting — the language toggle handler had no ISO to work from. Fix was three-part: (a) introduce `i18nLocale()` returning `'ar-EG'` or `'en-US'` based on `<html lang>`; (b) introduce `fmtTs(isoOrString)` that parses via `new Date(…)` and calls `toLocaleString(i18nLocale())`, falling back to the raw string if parsing fails (backward compatible); (c) change the save site to `new Date().toISOString()` and make `updateFetchTimestamp()` call `fmtTs(ts)` before display. `applyLang()` then re-invokes `updateFetchTimestamp(savedTs)` on every toggle, so `Fetched: 4/19/2026, 3:19:52 PM` instantly becomes `تم الجلب: ١٩‏/٤‏/٢٠٢٦، ٣:١٩:٥٢ م`. Also updated 10 other `toLocale*()` callsites (insight timestamps, cache time, fetch completion, print header, iteration formatter) to pass `i18nLocale()` instead of defaulting to browser locale or hardcoded `'en-US'`. **Why:** Persistent representation and display representation are two different concerns. `localStorage`, database columns, JSON payloads, and URL parameters should hold raw data (ISO strings, numeric cents, raw codes). Locale / number / currency formatting is a render-time concern that depends on the user's *current* language preference, which can change after the data was stored. Coupling them means every locale toggle requires re-fetching or re-computing the underlying data — a slow, brittle path. **How to apply:** For every bilingual or multi-locale application: (1) never call `toLocaleString()`, `toLocaleDateString()`, `toLocaleTimeString()`, `toLocaleString()`, `Intl.NumberFormat`, `Intl.DateTimeFormat` without explicitly passing the current locale; (2) for data that persists beyond one request (localStorage, API payload, DB row), store ISO 8601 dates / raw numbers / locale-independent codes; (3) add a single `i18nLocale()` helper that reads the current UI language and returns the appropriate BCP-47 tag — every formatting call goes through it; (4) wire the language toggle handler to re-run any function that renders persisted data so formatting updates immediately on toggle; (5) Grep periodically for `toLocale[A-Z]\w*\(\)` without arguments to catch regressions.

439. **When modeling team rosters, plan for "people who left" from day one — not as an after-the-fact patch** — On 2026-04-20, the Team Velocity tab initially assumed every name in `ASSIGNEE_TO_TEAM` represented a current employee. When user said "Badr became no longer in our company, like Eslam", the existing model had no way to represent it. Had to retrofit: (a) add a `FORMER_MEMBERS` set of display names; (b) add a `decorateName(name)` helper that appends `<span style="…">(former)</span>` when matched; (c) apply `decorateName()` to every render site where a name appears (Per-Member table, Members popup, Top contributors in the sprint tree, Team Roster cards); (d) add `'former' → 'سابق'` translation key so the marker flips bilingually. Their past closed tasks *still count* toward team velocity (historical accuracy is preserved) — the only change is the visual marker. A separate "Team Roster" reference section was also needed because former members with zero *current* closed tasks didn't appear in velocity tables at all. **Why:** Team rosters are almost always modelled as a flat {name → team} map with no lifecycle dimension. But real organisations have joiners, leavers, role changes, and dual-role/cross-team people. The first time a former employee appears in historical data (tasks, bugs, commits, timesheets), the naive model breaks down: they show up with a team label as if they're still current, or they disappear entirely because they have no current activity. Either outcome misrepresents the organisation to stakeholders. Retrofitting this after velocity / capacity / performance displays are built is painful because every render site needs updating. Planning for it from day one is a 5-minute addition: one `FORMER_MEMBERS` set + one `decorateName()` helper + one translation key + systematic application. **How to apply:** For any dashboard that lists team members (velocity, capacity, roster, org chart, performance review, timesheet): (1) model `TEAM_ROSTER` with the full canonical name list per team *and* a separate `FORMER_MEMBERS` set listing departures; (2) provide a `decorateName()` helper that attaches the "(former)" marker when matched; (3) use it at every render site — don't spread raw names directly into HTML; (4) bilingual marker text goes in the T map; (5) in velocity/throughput calculations, keep former members' historical contributions counted toward team totals (truthful history) but clearly visually marked so stakeholders don't plan future capacity around them; (6) if former members drop out of current-activity views entirely, add a separate "Team Roster" reference section that always shows the full org regardless of activity. Consider extending later to richer lifecycle states (joiner, on-leave, part-time, contractor) using the same pattern.

## Personal PM Prep Doc & Role-Verification Discipline (Apr 20, 2026)

440. **Verify team roles against the department-workflow source-of-truth BEFORE writing ownership or handoff statements into any deliverable** — On 2026-04-20, while building v3/v4 of the BMS Aqraboon-integration PM prep HTML, I routed PBI creation to Ahmed Lakosha ("Implementation / QA" per the 15-Apr MOM DOCX attendee list) and QA-QC approval to "Shehab / Hala". Both were wrong. Weekly Meeting Agenda roster + Department Workflows V4 Roles section confirmed: **Mahmoud Afify** is Implementation Team Rep (owns PBI creation per Sec.6.1 Request-to-Delivery), **Ahmed Lakosha** is Frontend Team Rep, **Mostafa Ahmed** is the sole QA-QC team member, **Shehab Gamal** is Backend Team Rep. The MOM DOCX's "Implementation / QA" label for Lakosha was stale; I inferred current roles from stale source text instead of the authoritative workflow doc. Same class of error in the same artefact: v4 said "confirm the Aqraboon project exists on dev.azure.com/TaqaTechno — if missing, ping Hatem before the call to create it" even though the project `KhairGate (BMS)` had existed under TaqaTechno for a year as a High-priority portfolio item. User caught both mistakes, not me. **Why:** A PM prep doc is an instrument for assigning commitments in a meeting — if the owner is wrong, the ask lands on the wrong person, and in the room it reads as a credibility hit (the PM doesn't know their own team). Role-verification is not a nice-to-have; it is a prerequisite for every sentence that names a person. **How to apply:** Before finalising any deliverable that contains a handoff statement, run a verification pass: for every named role or ownership phrase, (a) find the person in the authoritative source-of-truth for the project (Department Workflows V3/V4 + Weekly Meeting Agenda attendee list + Active Projects Portfolio), (b) confirm their current role, (c) confirm the system they work in is in the expected state (project already exists? access level correct?). When RAG returns a role statement, trust it over the MOM or email. If inconsistent between a MOM and the workflow doc, the **workflow doc wins** and the MOM gets flagged as stale. Applies to: PBI ownership assignments, QA approval handoffs, DevOps project provisioning steps, access-upgrade recommendations, closing-loop email recipient lists.

441. **Personal PM prep HTML is a separate artefact class from stakeholder-facing reports — different voice, different structure, different audience discipline** — On 2026-04-20, after delivering the stakeholder-facing v3 BMS-Aqraboon MOM HTML (6 tabs, neutral voice, source-faithful), the PM asked for a separate meeting-day playbook "for me only". What worked: a red 🔒 "For Mahmoud Only — do not circulate" badge in the header; first-person voice ("I", "me"); 5-tab structure mapped to meeting phases (Today's Plan / Agenda & Script / DevOps Setup / Risks & Questions I Must Ask / After the Meeting); speaking scripts as italic quote-cards for politically sensitive moments ("Presenting the reclassification without stepping on Salah", "Asking Aya for the Wallet ETA"); an explicit **Things I Should NOT Say** card paired with a **Say Instead** card; a **Political Sensitivities** card naming people and what might go wrong ("Bahaa carries business sign-off — don't ask him for technical commitments"; "Mostafa is a 1-person QA team — confirm capacity, don't just announce the SLA"); explicit **Green-Exit vs Red-Exit** outcomes at the top so success and failure are defined before the meeting starts. Filename prefix `PM_Personal_Meeting_Prep_vN.html` saved alongside the meeting-source artefacts (MOM DOCX, follow-up .msg) — distinct enough that it is visually obvious which files travel to stakeholders and which stay private. **Why:** Stakeholder HTMLs are credibility artefacts — neutral language, no political commentary, source-faithful rendering. Prep docs are the opposite — they NEED the political commentary, the "credit Salah publicly", the "watch out for Mostafa's capacity gap", the "never say the MOM got it wrong". Mixing these two genres into one file means either the stakeholder view is inappropriately candid, or the prep view is uselessly sanitised. Splitting them lets each be maximally useful for its audience. **How to apply:** Every time a PM has a meeting where they are running the room (kickoff, review, closing-loop, sprint retro, handover), produce a separate PM Personal Prep HTML alongside the stakeholder MOM/agenda. Use the Tab 1–5 pattern (Today / Agenda+Script / DevOps / Risks+Questions / After). Include speaking scripts for at minimum: the opening, presenting any analysis you did, asking the critical single-blocker question, closing the call. Include a Green-Exit / Red-Exit pair at the top so success is defined before the meeting starts. Always name it with the `PM_Personal_*` prefix and carry the 🔒 badge — these conventions make "do not share" visually obvious. Pair with lesson #442 (live-capture bridge) for the meeting-time capture dimension.

442. **Add form-field capture + localStorage auto-save + export-to-text button to turn a static prep doc into an in-meeting capture tool that feeds the minutes email directly** — On 2026-04-20, v6 of the BMS PM prep HTML contained a "Questions I must get answers to" table (8 rows) and an "18-PBI draft" table (18 rows) but they were read-only — the PM had no way to capture answers / owners / dates / decisions as the meeting progressed. User asked: "add the field for every action when I move through the meeting today, to make sure I can close all gaps". v7 added three ingredients: (1) **form fields on every actionable row** — for the 8 questions, columns Answer (textarea) / Committed-by (text) / Date (date picker) / Status (select with Answered·Date-to-answer·Parked·Blocked); for the 18 PBIs, columns Owner (text) / Afify Decision (select with Keep·Merge·Split·Rename·Drop) / Notes (text). Each input carries a unique `data-field` id and human-readable `data-label`. (2) **Auto-save to `localStorage` on every input/change event**, keyed under a versioned single key `pm-prep-v7-capture` holding a `{fieldId: value}` object. Visual feedback via toggling a `.filled` CSS class (amber background → green with green border). A **floating bar fixed bottom-right** shows "📝 Captured: X / Y" updating on every keystroke, with Export and Clear buttons. (3) An **Export button** that scans all `.capture` elements, groups them by their parent `<tbody>`'s `data-capture-section` attribute, emits a plaintext block with ISO timestamp and "=" separator, shown in a modal with a **📋 Copy to clipboard** button. The exported text is deliberately formatted to paste straight into the minutes email — grouped headings, bullet rows, one line per field. A Clear button with `confirm()` prompt lets the PM reset between meetings. Total: 86 capture fields across 2 tables, all auto-persisted, all exportable in one click. **Why:** A static prep doc stops being useful the moment the meeting starts. During the call, a PM can't track 8 questions × 4 fields + 18 PBIs × 3 fields in their head, and typing into a separate notes app loses the spatial context (which field goes with which row). Fields in the prep doc itself are spatial — they carry meaning-by-placement. Auto-save-to-localStorage means the PM can close the tab, switch to the DevOps tab in another window, come back, and the captured data is still there. Export-to-text is the real payoff: the prep doc IS the draft of the minutes email after the meeting ends — a one-click bridge from prep → execution → minutes that was previously three manual steps. **How to apply:** For any meeting-day prep doc where the PM will capture answers / decisions / owners / dates during the call, add: (a) form fields on every actionable row with unique `data-field` ids and human-readable `data-label` for export; (b) a `data-capture-section` attribute on each `<tbody>` to group the export by section; (c) JS that on `input`/`change` saves to `localStorage` under a versioned key, toggles a `.filled` class for visual feedback, and updates a floating `.capture-bar` counter; (d) an Export modal that reads localStorage, groups by section, and renders plaintext with `📋 Copy to clipboard`; (e) a Clear button with `confirm()`. Field types: `<textarea rows="2">` for free-text answers; `<input type="text">` for names; `<input type="date">` for deadlines; `<select>` for status/decision enums. Pair with lesson #441's private-playbook pattern — this bridge technique applies specifically to personal PM prep docs, not stakeholder-facing HTMLs (where filling fields in place is inappropriate because stakeholders receive the artefact read-only). This is distinct from lessons #60 (localStorage snapshots for trend tracking) and #127 (persist in-memory data) — the novel contribution is the **export-to-text bridge** that makes the prep doc feed the next deliverable automatically.

## V3 Dashboard Build & Bilingual Display Polish (Apr 20, 2026 — continued)

443. **Never nest `<script>` tags inside another `<script>`; HTML5 terminates the outer block at the first inner `</script>`** — On 2026-04-20, while extending `Project Management Handling_v3.html` with an embedded snapshots feature, a helper Node script injected `<script type="application/json" id="portfolio-apr16-diff">{...}</script>` plus a follow-up `<script>...snapshot JS...</script>` *between* `var PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES = {...};` and `function generateAutoText(...)`, both sitting inside an existing IIFE-wrapped outer `<script>`. The page rendered blank Portfolio cards. Playwright console surfaced `ReferenceError: PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES is not defined`. Root cause: HTML5's "script data" parser is raw-text except it scans for the literal string `</script>` to close the block. My injected `</script>` terminated the OUTER script prematurely, leaving a stray `<script type="application/json">` opening tag as a JS syntax error inside the outer block — the whole outer script fails to parse, so `PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES` never evaluates and every downstream consumer breaks. Fix was a follow-up Node script `_fix_snapshot_injection.js` that cut the injection from its bad position and reinserted it AFTER the outer script's closing `</script>` tag. **Why:** The `<script>` element's raw-text parsing is unintuitive — the HTML parser doesn't understand JS syntax, only the literal ASCII `</script>` string. This means any tool that mechanically inserts content into a large self-contained HTML must know where the outer script boundaries are, because nested `<script>` tags *look* fine visually but destroy the outer block. **How to apply:** (1) When writing a tool that injects scripts or embedded JSON into an existing HTML, first identify the enclosing outer `<script>` boundaries (find the preceding `<script>` tag and its matching `</script>`). Insert strictly outside those boundaries — typically just before `</body>`. (2) If you *must* embed within a string, escape every `</script>` as `<\/script>` (the backslash is ignored by JS but not by the HTML parser). (3) Verify with Playwright: navigate to the page, evaluate `typeof PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES`. If the answer isn't `'object'`, there is a parse error you can't see without Chrome DevTools. See also KB entry `pm-html-infrastructure/SKILL.md` which flags this as a standing rule.

444. **When injecting code that consumes IIFE-scoped variables, add explicit `window.X = X;` exports at the end of the IIFE** — On 2026-04-20, after fixing the nested-script bug (lesson #443) by moving the snapshot injection outside the outer `<script>`, the page STILL threw `ReferenceError: PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES is not defined` from the new snapshot code. The outer dashboard script was wrapped in `(function() { ... })();` (IIFE) starting at line 2427 and closing at line 7869, and `var PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES = {...};` declared inside the IIFE was function-scoped — invisible to any `<script>` block sitting after line 7869. Resolution: inserted five `window.X = X;` lines just before the closing `})();` — `window.PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES = PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES;`, `window.loadPortfolioText = ...`, `window.loadPortfolioStatus = ...`, `window.buildPortfolioProjects = ...`, `window.dgT = ...`. After the page reload, Playwright confirmed `typeof window.PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES === 'object'` and the snapshot feature rendered 27 project narratives correctly. **Why:** `var` at the top level of a `<script>` tag (no IIFE) creates a window global. Inside an IIFE, `var` is function-scoped — it's encapsulation working as intended, but also a scope barrier that new code added outside the IIFE cannot cross without an explicit bridge. The fix is trivial (5 lines), but the diagnosis takes a few minutes if you don't know to look for an IIFE boundary. **How to apply:** Before injecting any code into a single-file HTML dashboard that references an existing variable: (1) grep for `(function\s*\(\s*\)\s*\{` or `\}\)\s*\(\s*\)\s*;` to detect IIFE wrapping; (2) if found, add `window.X = X;` exports for every consumed var at the end of the IIFE; (3) note that functions already assigned as `window.X = function() {...}` ARE global — only bare `var`/`const`/`let`/`function` declarations need bridging; (4) if the codebase already exposes many helpers (like this dashboard did with `showToast`, `renderPortfolioStatus`, etc.), follow the same convention — don't rely on the scope closure alone.

445. **Watch for dual encoding in hand-written JS source: some strings use literal `—` while others use `\u2014` (6-char) escape** — On 2026-04-20, while refreshing `PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES` narratives in v3, direct `Edit` tool replacements on the English fields kept failing with "String to replace not found" even though the string was visually present. `od -c` on the file revealed English strings were written as 6-char escapes `\`, `u`, `2`, `0`, `1`, `4` while Arabic strings in the same block used literal UTF-8 `—` em-dash. The Edit tool's `old_string` parameter passes through a JSON-style decoder that interprets `\u2014` as the single em-dash character — so my `old_string` contained a 1-char em-dash while the file had 6 ASCII chars. They don't match. Workaround: wrote a Node script (`_apply_apr20_refresh.js`) that composed replacement strings using template literals with `EM = '\\u2014'` (escaped backslash), used JS `indexOf` / `slice` to locate and replace each narrative block byte-exact. Node doesn't decode anything; template literals preserve bytes as typed. **Why:** File tools commonly have a JSON-layer escape interpretation between "what you typed in the tool" and "what lands in `old_string`". Files hand-written over years often mix literal Unicode chars with `\uXXXX` JS escapes depending on the keyboard used at the time — there is no guarantee of consistency even within one file. Mechanical find-and-replace silently fails when encoding assumptions mismatch. **How to apply:** (1) Before bulk-editing strings containing non-ASCII in an unfamiliar file, run `grep -c '\\u' FILE` and `grep -c '—' FILE` to detect whether the file uses escapes or literals — if BOTH have non-zero counts, you have mixed encoding. (2) For mixed-encoding files, prefer a small Node script over direct `Edit` — Node's template literals `\`...\${EM}...\`` with `EM = '\\u2014'` preserves bytes exactly. (3) After a replace, verify with `od -c FILE | grep 'u2014'` or similar so you know which form survived. (4) Prefer writing new narratives with only ONE form (usually literal UTF-8) to avoid perpetuating the mix.

446. **For fresh emails not yet in the CSV export, decode `.msg` files via PowerShell Outlook COM** — On 2026-04-20, the user referenced an Alaqraboon "on hold" letter sent Apr 19 at 17:07 from Mohamed Salah Basha to Mohammed Abdulla Al-Yafei + Dr. Amar Bendou. The standard Outlook CSV category files (`cat_khairgate.csv`, etc.) were last exported at Apr 16 13:57 and didn't contain anything after that timestamp. A Grep of `D:\` surfaced the exact `.msg` file at `D:\Alaqraboon\Email\الأقربون - تأجيل التنفيذ - مقترح كتاب موجه لسعادة الرئيس التنفيذي لقطر الخيرية.msg`. Decoded via: `powershell.exe -NoProfile -Command "[Console]::OutputEncoding = [System.Text.Encoding]::UTF8; $ol = New-Object -ComObject Outlook.Application; $msg = $ol.Session.OpenSharedItem('D:\\Alaqraboon\\Email\\...msg'); @('SUBJECT: ' + $msg.Subject, 'FROM: ' + $msg.SenderName, 'TO: ' + $msg.To, 'DATE: ' + $msg.SentOn, '---BODY---', $msg.Body) | Out-File -FilePath 'D:\\Alaqraboon\\Email\\_extracted_body.txt' -Encoding UTF8"`. Then `Read` on the UTF-8 text file gave full English metadata + full Arabic body text — a critical portfolio input (project ON HOLD) that reshaped the Alaqraboon Portfolio Status card. **Why:** Outlook CSV exports are point-in-time snapshots; any email arriving after the export is invisible to text-only tooling. Binary `.msg` files (OLE compound documents) are not plain-text greppable without a parser. PowerShell + Outlook COM gives native access to `Subject`, `SenderName`, `To`, `SentOn`, `Body`, `Attachments`, etc. with full Unicode support as long as you write to a UTF-8 file (the console codepage mangles Arabic to `?`). **How to apply:** For any PM analysis where the CSV cutoff is older than the user's question: (1) `Glob` / `Grep` the local filesystem for `.msg` files matching keywords; (2) extract with the PowerShell one-liner above, saving to a scratch `_extracted_body.txt`; (3) `Read` the text file; (4) clean up the scratch file when done. Requires Outlook installed on the local machine (normally true for PMs). Pair with lesson #436 (search local data before saying "not in KB") — a `.msg` file on disk is authoritative local data the KB doesn't see.

447. **Threshold-color the *number* itself, not just the card's border** — On 2026-04-20, user pointed out that the v3 SPI/CPI summary cards showed 0.94 and 0.74 in dark navy text even though the underlying `indicatorColor()` function existed and was being called — but only to set `el.style.borderLeftColor`. The big 32px value stayed the default dark color. Fix: after the two `borderLeftColor` assignments, added two more lines: `document.getElementById('exec-overall-spi').style.color = indicatorColor(tot.spi, 'spi');` and same for CPI. After reload, SPI 0.94 rendered green (≥0.80) and CPI 0.74 rendered red (<0.80) — instantly readable without reading the label. **Why:** Visual hierarchy on a dashboard follows a simple rule — the reader's eye lands on the largest, boldest, most-colored element first. If the color coding lives on the frame (border) instead of the focal content (number), stakeholders have to second-guess the color's meaning. A red left-border around a black "0.74" reads as ambiguous; a red "0.74" with a red left-border reads unambiguously as a red indicator. The fix is typically one or two lines per tile. **How to apply:** For every "traffic-light" indicator in a dashboard (SPI, CPI, % complete, budget ratio, risk score, health rollup): the visible number MUST take the threshold color, not the surrounding chrome. Border / background colors are secondary reinforcement. Periodically Grep the codebase for `style.borderLeftColor` or `.style.borderColor` and audit whether each also has a matching `.style.color` on the text inside the same element. Exception: if the number is part of a multi-column table row, color the cell background OR the number; keep it consistent across the column.

448. **The PM Snapshot audit-trail pattern — Save Snapshot button on the live tab + separate History tab with dated locked entries** — On 2026-04-20, user clarified a board-reporting need: "every bi-weekly review cycle needs its own frozen snapshot so I can reproduce what I showed the board on any past date". Solution shipped in v3: (a) a **📌 Save Snapshot** button in the Portfolio Status tab header that prompts for a label (default "Bi-Weekly Review — <today>") and appends a locked JSON entry to `localStorage.exec-portfolio-snapshots-v1`; (b) a new **📜 Project Status (History)** sidebar tab with a table of all snapshots (date · label · author · project count · locked/draft badge · View / Export / Delete actions); (c) two snapshots embedded in the HTML at build time via `<script type="application/json" id="portfolio-apr16-diff">{...}</script>` — one for the Apr 16 seed (preserved as a diff of the 5 refreshed projects' old text) + one for Apr 20 refresh (full PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES) — so the HTML ships board-ready with zero configuration. View opens a read-only modal rendering all 27 project cards as they were at that snapshot date. **Why:** Without dated snapshots, every narrative refresh silently overwrites history — the PM cannot answer "what did we tell the board two weeks ago" without rummaging through old email threads. The board-review cycle IS the calendar of record; each cycle deserves a frozen baseline like PMI Earned Value uses. Embedded snapshots (in the HTML itself) beat external JSON files for this use case because emailing the HTML = emailing the full audit trail in one file. localStorage is the right target for in-session saves because the dashboard is file://, so it survives page reloads but not device switches — a separate **Export All Snapshots** button covers the cross-device case. **How to apply:** For any stakeholder-facing narrative dashboard that will be reviewed on a recurring cadence (bi-weekly, monthly, quarterly): (1) add a prominent Save button on the editing tab — tied to the review cycle, not to every DevOps fetch; (2) add a separate History tab showing a dated table of saved snapshots with View / Export actions; (3) embed existing snapshots as `<script type="application/json">` blocks in the HTML so new deployments inherit the history; (4) treat published snapshots as immutable (read-only, no edit) — corrections go in the NEXT snapshot with a "Correction from <prev date>" note; (5) the snapshot object shape is `{date, label, publishedBy, publishedAt, locked:true, narratives: {...}, statuses: {...}, metricsAtPublish?}` — `metricsAtPublish` captures live numbers at publish time so the compare view can show deltas even after DevOps data moves on.

449. **Use real Unicode characters in translation values, never HTML entities (`&Sigma;`, `&mdash;`, `&times;`)** — On 2026-04-20, v3's new EVM glossary in AR mode rendered `&Sigma; OriginalEstimate` on-screen literally. The i18n engine's `applyLang()` walker does `el.textContent = T[key]`, and `textContent` sets raw text — it does NOT decode HTML entities. Fix: replaced `&Sigma;` in the three `evm-ev-def` / `evm-pv-def` / `evm-ac-def` translation values with the actual Unicode character `Σ` (U+03A3, `\u03a3` in escaped form). After reload, AR rendered `(Σ OriginalEstimate حيث الحالة Done/Closed)` correctly. **Why:** HTML entities are an HTML-parser-layer concern. When an i18n framework bypasses the HTML parser (by setting `textContent` or `innerText`), entities stay as literal ampersand-sequences. The same issue bites `innerHTML`-based frameworks too if the entity appears in an attribute or data field that later gets `textContent`'d. Translation dictionary values are *data*; HTML entities are a *presentation* layer. Mixing them creates a presentation assumption that breaks the moment the framework changes its rendering path. **How to apply:** (1) When writing translation values (either EN or AR or any language), always use real Unicode characters: `Σ` not `&Sigma;`, `—` not `&mdash;`, `×` not `&times;`, `→` not `&rarr;`, `≥` not `&ge;`, etc. (2) When auditing existing translation dictionaries, Grep for `&[a-zA-Z]+;` inside string values and replace each with its Unicode equivalent. (3) HTML entities in the HTML *structure* (outside translation values) are fine — they get parsed by the HTML parser normally. The rule is scoped to values that will pass through `textContent`. (4) This is an extension of lesson #437 (no HTML tags in `data-i18n` values) — same root cause, different symptom.

450. **Always provide the full name of an acronym in the glossary, with the acronym in parentheses** — On 2026-04-20, user reviewed v3's "How to read" legend and flagged: "Earned Value Management - EVM, full name, not shortcut, for all". Original glossary led with acronyms: `EV (Earned Value) — ...`, `PV (Planned Value) — ...`. Revised to lead with full names: `Earned Value (EV) — ...`, `Planned Value (PV) — ...`, `Actual Cost (AC) — ...`. Section title changed from "EVM Glossary" to "Earned Value Management (EVM) Glossary". The in-legend SPI/CPI formulas changed from `SPI = EV / PV` to `Schedule Performance Index (SPI) = Earned Value (EV) / Planned Value (PV)`. Same pattern applied in AR: `مؤشر أداء الجدول الزمني (SPI) = القيمة المُكتسَبة (EV) / القيمة المخطّطة (PV)`. The top-of-page summary cards stayed compact (`SPI = EV / PV`) because space is tight — only the glossary block where the user reads for context gets the full expansion. **Why:** Board members and cross-functional stakeholders (finance, legal, HR) rarely memorise PM / EVM acronyms. When they see "SPI 0.94" on a dashboard, they glance at the legend to confirm what SPI measures. A legend that says "SPI = EV / PV" recursively offloads the interpretation cost — now the reader has to resolve three acronyms instead of one. Leading with the full name cuts the chain to one step. The compact cards preserve fast scanning; the glossary preserves correctness. **How to apply:** (1) Every glossary section leads with **Full Name (Acronym)** per row — never just the acronym alone. (2) The section *title* follows the same pattern (`Earned Value Management (EVM) Glossary`, not `EVM Glossary`). (3) In-context formulas in the glossary expand the acronyms: `Schedule Performance Index (SPI) = Earned Value (EV) / Planned Value (PV)`. (4) Summary tiles, table column headers, and sparkline tooltips stay compact (acronym only) — the glossary carries the expansion. (5) In bilingual dashboards, the Arabic glossary row includes both Arabic full name AND the English acronym — e.g., `القيمة المُكتسَبة — Earned Value (EV)` — so the reader can cross-reference literature in either language.

451. **In RTL dashboards, do NOT override the flex header with `flex-direction: row-reverse`; it cancels the natural RTL flip** — On 2026-04-20, user showed a screenshot of v3 in Arabic mode with the title/subtitle at the left and the user-info card (Mahmoud's name, company, last-updated) at the right — opposite of what the Arabic reading direction expects (title at right = reading start). The culprit was a single CSS rule: `html[dir="rtl"] .header { flex-direction: row-reverse; }`. The intent had been "flip the header for RTL", but the browser's natural RTL rendering already flips flex children — adding `row-reverse` flips them back to LTR order. Removing the rule, adding `text-align: right` on the title block and flipping the accent bar from `border-left` to `border-right` produced the correct layout: title on right (Arabic reading start), user-info on left (visual end). Playwright confirmed `titleBlockLeft=337, userInfoBlockLeft=28` in AR mode. **Why:** Flexbox in RTL mode already mirrors children visually when `dir="rtl"` is set on an ancestor — first child goes to the right, last child goes to the left. Applying `flex-direction: row-reverse` on top of this cancels the flip (two negatives = positive), which is almost never what you want. This is a common reflex error: developers who think "RTL ⇒ I need to reverse" reach for `row-reverse` when the browser has already done the work. **How to apply:** (1) For every flex container styled inside `html[dir="rtl"]`, remove `flex-direction: row-reverse` unless you have explicitly tested that the default RTL flip goes the WRONG way for your use case (rare). (2) Handle text alignment and border accents separately: inside `html[dir="rtl"]`, add `text-align: right` (or `text-align: start` for direction-agnostic styling) on text-heavy children, and flip `border-left` to `border-right` + swap `padding-left`/`padding-right`. (3) For vertical alignment in a flex column, `align-items: flex-end` flips automatically in RTL (flex-end becomes left), so usually no override is needed. (4) Verify with Playwright: navigate, switch to AR, call `document.querySelector('.header').children[0].getBoundingClientRect()` — the first child's `left` should be > the last child's `left` in RTL. (5) Related to lesson #437 (no HTML tags inside translation values) and lesson #438 (ISO timestamps, render-time locale) — all three are part of the bilingual-dashboard discipline that must be audited together.

452. **Audit `localStorage` keys before answering "will this button erase my data?"** — On 2026-04-20, early in the session, the user asked: "if I click Fetch from DevOps, will the data in Portfolio Status be removed?" Rather than guessing, Grep'd the code for every `localStorage.setItem` / `removeItem` call and produced a clean answer: Fetch from DevOps writes to `okr-kpi-*` and `okr-data-gate-snapshot` keys; Portfolio Status uses an independent `exec-portfolio-text-v1` + `exec-portfolio-status-v1` pair; there is zero overlap, so the user's Where/Next/Risk edits survive any Fetch. Even the Clear button's reach was traced — it only wipes `STORAGE_KEY` + `FETCH_TS_KEY`, leaving Portfolio Status edits intact. **Why:** "Will this action destroy my work?" is one of the most common and highest-anxiety questions a PM asks about their own dashboard. A wrong answer ("yes, it'll overwrite") scares the user into avoiding features they need; a wrong answer ("no, it's safe") can cost them hours of typed-in narrative if it turns out to be false. The answer is always discoverable by tracing storage keys — feature boundaries typically align with storage-key boundaries because each feature was built by someone who namespaced their own state. Give a precise, trace-based answer, not a reassurance. **How to apply:** When asked whether action A will mutate data B, (1) Grep for `localStorage.setItem` / `sessionStorage.setItem` / `indexedDB` writes inside A's handler; (2) compare the key names to the storage keys that hold B; (3) if no overlap, answer "no" with the key names as proof; (4) if overlap exists, explain exactly which fields get overwritten and suggest an export-first workflow. Use this same pattern for any "does X affect Y" question about shared state — cache clears, bulk operations, auth resets.

453. **Keep historical KR / metric queries pinned to the historical roster — don't let team reassignments rewrite past performance** — On 2026-04-20, moving Houssem Ben Mbarek + Muram Makawi Abuzaid from Qatar team → Backend team (v2 edit) touched six code sites: rendered rows, `TEAM_ROSTER`, `ASSIGNEE_TO_TEAM`, `teamDefs` in `fetchMetrics`, `memberDefs`, and `teamInfoMap`. All six followed the new roster. BUT the `qatarAllTimeDone` array (used for KR3.1 "Qatar members with 10+ done items") was deliberately LEFT pointing at `['Houssem', 'Muram', 'Hacene']` — the three members who were on Qatar during the historical window the KR measures. If `qatarAllTimeDone` had also been rewritten to just `['Hacene']`, the KR would drop from a potential 3/3 to a permanent 1/3 — making the team look like it underperformed when in reality it was just restructured. This insight was noted in the CLAUDE.md v2 entry as a deliberate choice. **Why:** KRs and retrospective metrics measure performance within a time window. Changing team membership today should not retroactively rewrite who "counted" yesterday. Coupling the metric's member list to the current roster introduces survivorship / restructuring bias into historical comparisons — stakeholders lose the ability to compare Q1 velocity to Q2 velocity if Q1's team members are no longer associated with their historical team in the metric code. **How to apply:** (1) When refactoring team / assignment maps, separate "current roster" (used for live velocity, capacity, current-sprint rollups) from "historical rosters" (used for KRs, trend lines, performance reports). (2) Give the historical lists explicit names like `qatarAllTimeDone` (suffix `AllTime` = pinned) to make the intent obvious to the next maintainer. (3) When a team restructure happens mid-quarter, consider whether any KR thresholds need adjustment (e.g., Qatar KR3.1 target was 3/3 — with 1 person left, target may need to move to 1/1 or the KR may need to be retired). Flag this as a PM decision, not an automatic code sync. (4) Document the split in the file's top comment or README so future refactorers don't "helpfully" unify the two lists.

454. **`localStorage` is per-file-origin on `file://` URLs — user edits in v2 don't automatically transfer to v3** — On 2026-04-20, cutting `Project Management Handling_v3.html` from v2 raised the question "how do I preserve the user's typed-in narrative edits from v2?" Answer: you can't, automatically. Browsers treat each `file://` path as its own security origin — `file:///d:/.../v2.html` and `file:///d:/.../v3.html` have separate `localStorage` sandboxes that cannot see each other. The user's typed-in Where/Next/Risk overrides in v2 stay locked to v2's origin. Workaround options evaluated: (a) pre-flight DevTools snippet the user runs in v2 to export to clipboard, pasted back into chat; (b) temporary "Export" button added to v2 that dumps state to a file; (c) build v3 with an "Import Legacy v2 State" input panel accepting the JSON; (d) skip migration entirely if the user hasn't edited anything. Chose (d) for this session because user confirmed the visible text was still the Apr 16 seed. **Why:** Many PMs assume "it's the same HTML folder, so the data flows". That assumption holds only if both files share the SAME origin — which they don't, by browser design. This is a hidden cost of the "new version every update" rule: each version starts with an empty localStorage for that specific file path. For small changes (display-only polish), in-place edits to the existing file preserve user data with zero migration. For cross-day / major-scope changes, accept that user data is orphaned in the old file and build a migration step. **How to apply:** (1) Before cutting a new versioned file, run a pre-flight check: does the user have localStorage data in the old version that they care about? (2) If yes, provide at least one of: a DevTools snippet to copy to clipboard, an "Export" button added to the old file, or an "Import" panel in the new file. (3) If the change is display-only (no schema change), prefer in-place edits — see lesson #458. (4) Document the origin-per-file behavior in the dashboard's own README/help tooltip so users aren't surprised. (5) Consider serving files from `localhost:PORT` (via a persistent Node server per lesson #455) so all versions share the same origin — a bigger architectural commitment but eliminates the migration problem entirely.

455. **Playwright blocks `file://` by default — spin up a Node one-liner HTTP server on a throwaway port to verify local HTMLs** — On 2026-04-20, attempting to navigate Playwright to `file:///.../Project Management Handling_v3.html` returned `Error: Access to "file:" protocol is blocked`. Chrome's security default disables `file://` for automated browsers. Workaround: `node -e "const http=require('http'),fs=require('fs'),path=require('path');const root=__dirname;http.createServer((req,res)=>{let f=path.join(root,decodeURIComponent(req.url.split('?')[0]));if(f.endsWith(path.sep))f=path.join(f,'index.html');fs.readFile(f,(e,d)=>{if(e){res.statusCode=404;res.end('404');return;}const ext=path.extname(f).toLowerCase();const ct={'html':'text/html;charset=utf-8','js':'application/javascript','json':'application/json','css':'text/css'}[ext.slice(1)]||'application/octet-stream';res.setHeader('Content-Type',ct);res.end(d);});}).listen(5099,()=>console.log('on :5099'));"` — run as a background process, then Playwright navigates to `http://localhost:5099/v3.html`. **Why:** Playwright and other headless-browser tools disable `file://` to reduce local-file-exfiltration attacks. The fix isn't a Playwright flag (most environments don't expose `--allow-file-access-from-files`) — it's serving the file over HTTP. A one-liner Node server is zero-install (Node is standard on PM workstations) and throwaway (different port for each session, killed with Ctrl+C). **How to apply:** (1) For any Playwright verification of a local HTML file, start the Node one-liner above in the background; pick a port unlikely to collide (5099 is free on most PM workstations per CLAUDE.md's enumeration of 5050-5053). (2) Navigate Playwright to `http://localhost:PORT/filename.html`. (3) Note that the localhost-HTTP origin will have EMPTY `localStorage` vs the user's `file://` localStorage — the two are different origins (see lesson #454). Use this for render verification and JS behavior testing; don't use it to poke at the user's actual localStorage state. (4) Kill the server when done (`kill %1` in the same shell, or let the session close). (5) If the file has external asset references (CSS, JS imports), make sure the Node server serves those too — the one-liner above does via the root-dir static serve.

456. **When a `<span data-i18n="key">text</span>` has trailing text AFTER the span in the same parent, that trailing text is invisible to the i18n engine** — On 2026-04-20, user spotted a legend line that read (in Arabic mode) `SPI = نسبة اكتمال النطاق (...). 1.0 = half complete = all scope closed.` — half translated, half English. The HTML source was `<div><strong>SPI</strong> = <span data-i18n="spi-formula">...</span>. 1.0 = all scope closed; 0.5 = half complete.</div>`. The `spi-formula` key translated correctly; but the period + rest (" 1.0 = all scope closed; 0.5 = half complete.") sits as a RAW text node AFTER the span in the same parent `<div>`. The i18n walker only touches elements with `data-i18n` attributes — bare text nodes between/after tagged spans are invisible. Fix: wrap the trailing text in a new `<span data-i18n="spi-values-legend">...</span>` and add the AR translation to the T dictionary. **Why:** Any i18n framework built on `data-i18n` attribute selectors has this blind spot. The fix is trivial per occurrence, but the drift accumulates across long-lived bilingual pages because contributors add "one more clause" inline to an existing translated sentence without wrapping it. **How to apply:** (1) Periodic audit pattern: Grep for `</span>[.]` or `</span>\s[A-Za-z0-9]` or `</span>\s*\([A-Z]` to catch trailing English after a `data-i18n` span. (2) Stricter rule: inside any element that contains at least one `data-i18n` child span, every text fragment must also be wrapped in a `data-i18n`-bearing element — no bare text nodes mixed with translated spans. (3) When adding a clause to an existing translated sentence, wrap it in a new span rather than appending raw text. (4) Consider adding a lint script that walks the DOM and flags any element containing both a `data-i18n` descendant AND a non-whitespace text node at the same level. (5) Related to lesson #437 (no HTML tags inside translation values) and lesson #449 (no HTML entities in translation values) — together these three lessons form the "i18n-clean data contract" for bilingual dashboards.

457. **Translate full noun phrases precisely: "Schedule" (الجدول) ≠ "Schedule Performance Index" (مؤشر أداء الجدول الزمني) — maintain a bilingual PM vocabulary** — On 2026-04-20, user caught that the AR translation for SPI said "مؤشر أداء الجدول" when the correct PM-literature form is "مؤشر أداء الجدول الزمني" — the "الزمني" (temporal / time-based) qualifier distinguishes "schedule" (as in time schedule) from "الجدول" (which alone could mean a data table). Without "الزمني", Arabic-speaking board members parse the term ambiguously. Two translation keys needed fixing: `'Schedule Performance Index'` → "مؤشر أداء الجدول الزمني" and `'spi-formula-title'` → "مؤشر أداء الجدول الزمني (SPI) = ...". CPI's "مؤشر أداء التكلفة" was already correct because "التكلفة" (cost) is unambiguous. **Why:** Literal first-word translations of English compound terms often drop context-essential qualifiers that the full phrase carries. PM / EVM / Agile vocabulary is especially prone to this because terms like "Schedule", "Burndown", "Velocity", "Story Point" carry discipline-specific meaning that single-word Arabic translations don't preserve. The cost of a wrong translation isn't just linguistic — it's stakeholder confusion during board review. **How to apply:** (1) Maintain a bilingual PM vocabulary reference — a markdown file mapping each PM/EVM/Agile term to its Arabic industry-standard translation (not a machine-translated word-for-word). Seed entries: SPI = مؤشر أداء الجدول الزمني; CPI = مؤشر أداء التكلفة; EV = القيمة المُكتسَبة; PV = القيمة المخطّطة; AC = التكلفة الفعلية; Sprint = سبرنت (transliterated) or تكرار (iteration); Velocity = سرعة الفريق; Burndown = الاحتراق التدريجي; KR = نتيجة رئيسية; Objective = هدف. (2) When adding a new bilingual string, look up the term in the vocabulary first — don't invent a translation on the fly. (3) For acronyms, keep the English acronym in parentheses alongside the Arabic full name (see lesson #450): `مؤشر أداء الجدول الزمني (SPI)`. (4) Periodically run a "PM jargon audit" — grep for known terms and verify every translation uses the agreed form. (5) Crosscheck with RAG results from PM²-Agile / PMP / PMBOK Arabic editions when available.

458. **Pragmatic in-place edits are allowed for same-day polish; version bumps are for cross-day or major-scope changes — set a "polish window" exception to the versioning rule** — On 2026-04-20, v3 shipped with the Portfolio refresh + snapshot feature. Over the next few hours, v3 absorbed ~10 follow-up fixes in-place without becoming v4/v5/v6/...: (a) untranslated suffix wrapping; (b) Σ entity fix; (c) number-color on SPI/CPI cards; (d) EVM terminology in cards + legend; (e) full acronym names per user feedback; (f) "الزمني" Arabic correction; (g) RTL header flex fix; (h) border-left → border-right accent bar; (i) alignment corrections. If each had triggered a new version file, the folder would now hold v3 through v12 within a single calendar day — unreadable sprawl. Decision: within a "polish window" (same calendar day, display-only changes, no schema/data/contract changes), edit in place; the standing "new version every update" rule kicks in for the NEXT cross-day change. **Why:** Versioning is a governance tool for traceability, not a mechanical rule for every keystroke. The real signal a version bump carries is "this is a distinct state worth referring back to". Ten incremental typo/style fixes in the same afternoon do NOT produce ten distinct states worth referring back to — they produce one polished end-of-day state. Mechanical versioning degrades the signal-to-noise ratio of the version log. **How to apply:** Define a polish-window exception to the "new version on every update" rule: **in-place edits are permitted when ALL of these hold** — (1) same calendar day as the version's original creation; (2) display-only changes (text, colors, translations, CSS) — no new data fields, no schema changes, no new localStorage keys, no new API calls; (3) no content changes that stakeholders would want to reference separately ("show me v3 before the EVM fix" is a sign you should've bumped to v4). Outside this window — cross-day changes, any data/schema/contract change, any content stakeholders might want to pin — bump the version per the standing rule. Document the polish-window edits as a dated CHANGELOG paragraph inside the HTML's top comment or the companion CLAUDE.md entry so traceability is preserved without file sprawl.

## Cadence Migration, i18n Edge Cases & CWD-Lock Workarounds (Apr 20, 2026 — continued)

459. **Audit BOTH literal-Unicode AND `\uXXXX` escape forms when bulk-replacing non-ASCII text in a single file** — On 2026-04-20, migrating the PM Handling dashboard from bi-weekly to weekly cadence required replacing Arabic phrases like "نصف الشهرية" → "الأسبوعية" and "3 مايو" → "26 أبريل". A single JS file mixed both forms: `PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES` entries used literal Unicode chars (the user typed them directly), while the T i18n dictionary used `\u0645\u0627\u064a\u0648`-style escape sequences (copy-pasted from older code generators). My first Node pass with literal-Arabic replacements caught ~70% of occurrences; the remaining ~30% needed a second pass targeting the escape form explicitly. Took 3 passes + 1 manual edit to reach 0 residual. **Why:** File tools see bytes, not characters. The literal-char form `\u064a\u0648` as Unicode scalar U+064A U+0648 (a 4-byte UTF-8 sequence) is COMPLETELY different from the ASCII escape sequence `\` + `u` + `0` + `6` + `4` + `A` + `u` + `0` + `6` + `4` + `8` (11 ASCII bytes). A string-replace targeting one form silently skips the other. Long-lived codebases accumulate both forms because different contributors/tools wrote them differently. **How to apply:** (1) Pre-flight grep: `grep -c "LITERAL_ARABIC_WORD" FILE` AND `grep -cE "\\\\u[0-9a-f]{4}.*\\\\u[0-9a-f]{4}" FILE` — if both non-zero, the file has mixed encoding. (2) For bulk rename, write TWO replacement tables: one for literal chars, one for `\u`-escape form. (3) Run both passes. (4) Final grep for residuals using the escape form of each target word (e.g. `\u0645\u0627\u064a\u0648` for مايو). (5) If you're generating new content, prefer ONE form (literal UTF-8 is more human-readable) and don't perpetuate the mix.

460. **Arabic (and other Semitic languages) gender/state inflections demand their own replacement rules — don't assume one form covers all** — On 2026-04-20, my cadence migration replacement table handled the feminine definite form `نصف الشهرية` but missed two other forms that appeared in the same file: the masculine definite `نصف الشهري` (no ة on the adjective — because "اجتماع" is masculine and agreement requires a masculine adjective) and the indefinite feminine `نصف شهرية` (without the definite article ال). A roadmap-footer sentence reading "يتم مراجعة التقدم في اجتماع 3 مايو نصف الشهري" (progress is reviewed in the May 3 bi-weekly meeting) survived both automated passes because the adjective was masculine (`الشهري` without ة) — my pattern was `نصف الشهرية` with ة. Required a targeted manual edit. **Why:** Semitic-language adjectives inflect for gender (masc/fem), state (definite/indefinite), number (singular/dual/plural), and case (nominative/accusative/genitive — in classical Arabic). A single English word like "bi-weekly" can correspond to 4+ Arabic surface forms depending on what noun it modifies: `نصف شهري / نصف شهرية / نصف الشهري / نصف الشهرية / نصف شهريان / نصف الشهريان / ...` Naive string-replace hits one form and leaves the rest. The same class of issue exists for Hebrew, French, German, Spanish (adjective agreement), Russian (case endings), etc. **How to apply:** (1) Before writing a bulk-rename script, grep the file for the ROOT noun (e.g., `شهري`, `أسبوعي`) not the full phrase — list every inflected form you find. (2) Build the replacement table with one pair per inflection: `(نصف الشهرية, الأسبوعية)`, `(نصف الشهري, الأسبوعي)`, `(نصف شهرية, أسبوعية)`, `(نصف شهري, أسبوعي)`. (3) After the automated pass, grep for the root noun one more time — any survivors are forms you missed. (4) For content PMs write themselves, keep an internal glossary of the forms each acronym takes (see lesson #457 on the bilingual PM vocabulary reference).

461. **Phrase-level replacement tables must include disambiguating context — a bare date or number match across hundreds of files corrupts calendar-week markers** — On 2026-04-20, the bi-weekly→weekly migration needed to change the next-review-target date "May 3" → "Apr 26". The v3 HTML contains "May 3" in 13 places, but only 11 were deadline references to update. The remaining 2 were calendar-week timeline rows: `<tr>W18 Apr 27 – May 3 Weekly update</tr>` and `<tr>W21–W22 May 18 – May 31 Weekly updates</tr>`. A bare `'May 3' → 'Apr 26'` find-and-replace would corrupt the W18 week into a nonsense `Apr 27 – Apr 26` span. Fix: every entry in the replacement table included disambiguating context — `'by May 3 review'`, `'by May 3.'`, `'Target ≥ 60% by May 3'`, `'Sunday May 3'`, `'May 3 bi-weekly review'` — never bare `'May 3'`. **Why:** A date string like "May 3" is semantically overloaded: it can be a deadline, a calendar week endpoint, a historical event marker, a meeting name, a sprint close, a milestone. Each semantic needs a different fate during bulk migration. The context before/after the date disambiguates. Naive global find-and-replace is the second-easiest way to silently corrupt data (after accidentally deleting something). **How to apply:** (1) Before writing any bulk-rename rule for a date, number, acronym, or name, grep the file for ALL occurrences of the bare string and categorize them by surrounding context. (2) Write one replacement pair per context ("by X review" vs "W## X – Y" vs "X Sprint start"), never a bare pair. (3) After the pass, grep for bare occurrences of the original — residual hits should all be false positives (leave-alone cases) or clear misses worth a manual review. (4) For calendar-week vs deadline disambiguation specifically: week markers follow the pattern `W## <date> – <date>` or `Week N (<date>)`; deadlines follow `by <date>`, `<date> review`, `<date> sprint close`. Grep for both patterns to pre-classify. (5) This is a stronger form of lesson #445 (dual-encoding audit) applied to semantic ambiguity instead of byte ambiguity.

462. **When renaming an i18n key, update THREE locations in one atomic pass: the HTML `data-i18n` attribute, the dictionary lookup key, and the translation value — drift between any two breaks the render** — On 2026-04-20, changing the next-review date broke multiple i18n lookups because I updated the visible English text (between `<span data-i18n="...">...</span>` tags) but forgot to update the `data-i18n` attribute VALUE (the lookup key) OR the dictionary entry KEY string. Result when toggling AR: the browser looked up `T['Estimation Accuracy from SBI tab ≥ 60% by Apr 26 review']`, found no match (the dictionary still had `T['Estimation Accuracy from SBI tab ≥ 60% by May 3 review']`), and fell back to showing the EN text verbatim — looking "not translated" instead of translated. Also saw the opposite symptom: dictionary KEY was updated but the AR VALUE still contained the old date — AR mode then showed an Arabic string with the WRONG date. Took 3 passes to align all three. **Why:** The i18n contract is a three-way identity: `<span data-i18n="KEY">EN_TEXT</span>` PLUS `T[KEY] = AR_TEXT`. If any one of (EN_TEXT, KEY, AR_TEXT) changes without the other two, the contract breaks. Most UI i18n frameworks have a "key-first" pattern where translations are looked up by key, so dictionary key and HTML attribute MUST match byte-for-byte; the EN visible text is somewhat decoupled but visually should match the key for consistency. **How to apply:** (1) Treat the three locations as one atomic unit in any find-and-replace script. For each rename, produce three rules: one targeting the HTML attribute value (`data-i18n="old"` → `data-i18n="new"`), one targeting the inner EN text, one targeting the dictionary key (`'old':` → `'new':`). (2) After the pass, grep for the OLD form across all three contexts — if any survive, the rename is incomplete. (3) Verify via Playwright: toggle to AR and confirm the AR text appears (not the EN fallback). If the EN text shows in AR mode, the key/attribute/dictionary-key don't match. (4) Also update the AR VALUE in the dictionary if it contained the old date/term — the AR text has its own domain-specific strings that need updating in parallel.

463. **Cadence changes ripple across 8+ artifact classes simultaneously — audit the full list before starting, not after** — On 2026-04-20, switching the PM Handling review cadence from bi-weekly to weekly touched 8 files plus 1 folder with a total of 124 text replacements, across 4 automated passes + manual fixes. Artifact classes involved: (1) filesystem folder names ("Bi-Weekly Project Reviews" → "Weekly Project Reviews"); (2) dashboard UI labels (`Weekly Review Charter`, button tooltips); (3) default dynamic values (`saveSnapshot()` prompt default); (4) embedded historical snapshot labels (Apr 20 embedded snapshot); (5) narrative commitment dates inside portfolio cards ("First calibration check at May 3 bi-weekly review" → "Apr 26 weekly review"); (6) companion HTML documents (Sunday Talking Points throughout); (7) markdown source docs (`_portfolio_narratives.md` section headers + index); (8) CLAUDE.md governance/context. Had I missed any one class, the dashboard would ship inconsistent — "Weekly Review Charter" with a "next review May 3" inside it. **Why:** A cadence change is really a policy/governance change. Policy changes ripple through every policy-derived artifact. In a PM/dashboard ecosystem, that's filesystem layout + UI text + default values + historical snapshots + narratives + companion docs + governance files. Humans undercount this reliably — they think "change the Charter card" and miss the other 7 classes. **How to apply:** (1) Before starting a cadence (or any policy) change, enumerate every artifact class that carries the policy: folder names, dashboard UI labels, default values, historical labels, narrative text, companion docs, governance files, schedule automation (Task Scheduler, cron). (2) For each class, list specific files and run an initial grep for the old term. (3) Build one replacement script that handles ALL classes in one pass rather than walking class-by-class — this avoids the "I forgot that file" trap. (4) Verify with a comprehensive grep for the OLD term at the end — zero residual hits across all classes means the migration is complete. (5) Apply to: cadence changes (bi-weekly↔weekly), version bumps (Q2→Q3), role restructures (team member moves), terminology changes (acronym renames), scope changes (adding/removing a project from the portfolio).

464. **Windows refuses to rename or move a folder if any process's current-working-directory (CWD) is set to that folder OR any subfolder within it — work around by moving CONTENTS rather than the folder itself** — On 2026-04-20, attempting to archive `D:\Project Management\Project management Quarter Plan\Projects Handling Review Meeting 6-4-2026\` with `mv` returned `Device or resource busy` even after killing all `node.exe` HTTP server processes holding the folder open. Root cause: the current Claude Code session had this folder set as its CWD (from the original VSCode workspace-open), and Windows NTFS holds a handle on the CWD of every running process. Even an in-place rename was blocked. Workaround that worked: create the archive target, then MOVE each TOP-LEVEL ITEM out of the folder one-by-one, leaving only the tool-state subfolders (`.claude/`, `.playwright-mcp/`) that the session was actively using. Result: 35 items relocated, folder shell remains with 2 hidden tool-state dirs, clean separation between "archived content" and "active session state". **Why:** Windows NTFS differs from POSIX filesystems in how it handles folder locks. On Linux/macOS, an inode's path is rename-safe even when a process has CWD there — the process just continues holding the OLD path via its inode reference. On Windows, NTFS locks folders that are any process's CWD, and the rename fails. This affects `mv`, `ren`, `robocopy /MOVE`, `PowerShell Rename-Item`, and even `rmdir`. Subprocess trees inherit the parent's CWD, so spawning `bash` or `powershell` from a shell rooted in the folder compounds the issue — every child process also has a lock. **How to apply:** (1) Before attempting to move/rename a folder that's currently open in any editor/IDE/terminal/debugger, identify which processes have it as their CWD. `handle.exe -p <pid>` (Sysinternals) or `PowerShell Get-Process | Where-Object { $_.Path -like "*folder*" }`. (2) If you CAN close those processes (plain terminals, build watchers), close them and retry. (3) If you CAN'T (your own editor session's state folder), move CONTENTS instead of the folder. Use a loop: for each top-level item, `mv item TARGET/`. Skip hidden tool-state subfolders like `.git`, `.claude`, `.playwright-mcp`, `.vscode`, `node_modules` only if they belong to the active session. (4) After the content move, the empty shell can be renamed/deleted from a DIFFERENT session (one whose CWD is elsewhere). (5) For automation scripts that MUST rename folders, set `cd /` or `cd ~` at the start so the script's own CWD isn't inside the target. (6) For VSCode + Claude Code specifically: the session's workspace root is the CWD — move content only, delete the shell from a fresh session opened elsewhere.

## Session Continuity & Closure Handoffs (Apr 21, 2026)

465. **Produce a session-closure handoff BEFORE the user closes VSCode — never after** — When the user signals any kind of session pause or close ("brb", "back in X min", "closing session", "resume later", "will close VSCode", "takecare from this action"), proactively emit a compact bookmark covering: (1) current working folder + active file(s); (2) active mode (plan mode / auto mode / skill in progress); (3) pending decisions awaiting user input; (4) hard deadlines or dates on the calendar; (5) a copy-pasteable resume prompt the user can paste into a fresh Claude Code session to restore context. **Why:** The user opens Claude Code sessions from inside VSCode. Closing VSCode ends the session with no auto-persistence of conversation state — the next launch starts cold. Without an explicit handoff, the user must re-explain the entire context on restart, which is expensive mid-deliverable (PMO dashboards, Outlook reports, Alaqraboon readiness work often span 3-5 sessions). The cost of writing a 6-line handoff before pause is trivial (≈30 seconds); the cost of reconstructing mid-deliverable context without one is 10-30 minutes of re-briefing and repeated exploration. Confirmed as a standing rule by the user on 2026-04-21, after I produced a handoff unprompted when they said "I'll be back in 10 min and will close the session" — user then instructed "anytime when I close VSCode … you should take care of this action." **How to apply:** (1) Trigger on any of these phrases or their variants: "brb", "back in X", "be right back", "step away", "close session", "closing VSCode", "pause", "resume later", "continue tomorrow", "takecare from this action". (2) Emit the handoff BEFORE the user actually leaves — use the gap between their pause message and their next action, not after they return. (3) Keep the handoff compact enough to fit one screen, but include the resume prompt verbatim so paste-back works with zero editing. (4) If plan mode or auto mode is active when the pause is announced, call it out explicitly in the handoff so the user knows to re-enter the same mode in the next session. (5) If a skill is mid-execution (e.g. `/PM_drift-fix`, `/PM-lessons-add`), note the skill name + phase so the next session can pick up without re-invoking. (6) Applies globally across ALL projects, not just the current folder — PMO, Outlook, OKR, Alaqraboon, Udemy, Claude Courses, etc. (7) Pair with per-project memory: write `feedback_session_closure_handoff.md` into `C:\Users\DELL\.claude\projects\<project>\memory\` so the rule persists across sessions even if this global lesson is not loaded. (8) The resume prompt should name the file paths, the deliverable version number, and the specific pending decision — generic "pick up where we left off" prompts are useless across a fresh session with no context.

## HTML Deliverable Defaults (Apr 21, 2026)

466. **Every HTML I generate must ship with a favicon on the FIRST save — never as a follow-up fix** — On 2026-04-21 I delivered 3 HR candidate-comparison HTMLs (QC, BA, Senior Backend) without favicons. The user had to ask for them after the fact. This is the third+ time this miss has occurred despite lesson #309 already stating "every HTML deliverable must include a favicon." **Why:** #309 is phrased as a *review* rule, so it doesn't fire during *creation*. Fresh HTML generation needs a creation-time default, not a post-hoc check. The cost of the miss is cheap per file but the user has flagged the repetition as frustrating — favicon omission now reads as "Claude isn't paying attention to the basics." **How to apply:** (1) Treat favicon as part of the base HTML template, alongside `<!DOCTYPE html>`, `<meta charset>`, `<meta viewport>`, `<title>`. If any of those four are present, the `<link rel="icon">` must also be present. (2) Default favicon = inline SVG data URI (no external file), placed immediately after `<meta viewport>` and before `<title>`. (3) Choose an icon appropriate for the document family: HR → "HR" monogram on teal `#0f766e`; OKR → "OKR" monogram; PMO → "PM"; etc. Reuse across sibling files so tab strips look coherent. (4) When editing an existing HTML without a favicon, add one proactively — don't wait to be asked. (5) When producing a batch of HTMLs in one session (e.g. 3 candidate comparisons), apply the same favicon to all of them in the same pass. (6) Supersedes #309's review framing. #309 still applies to legacy files not created by me; #466 applies to any HTML I author or materially rewrite.


## Team Directory Drift & Retroactive Propagation (Apr 21, 2026)

467. **When a user confirms an unmapped team member's role, propagate the fact across ALL registry files in one batch — and close any matching 'Current Gap Identified' flag** — On 2026-04-21, user confirmed Afthab P A (afthab@taqatechno.com) is a QC engineer dedicated to the KhairGate Wallet project. Before confirmation, his name appeared ONLY in Azure DevOps work items (PBI #3986 + Tasks #3987/#3988/#3989 KT Sessions + Bug #4293) — none of the downstream registries knew about him: Department Workflows V1-V4 role tables, DevOps_Teams.md, OKR & KPI Dashboard v7 team roster, Project Management Handling_v3 Team Velocity tab, and Azure DevOps Access Upgrade v6 roster. Worse, Department Workflows V4 (Feb 2, 2026) still carried a "Current Gap Identified: QA-QC team consists of only 1 member handling all testing across multiple projects. A Senior QC Engineer hiring has been approved (target: by end of March 2026)." This gap flag was stale for ≥19 days because Afthab's Apr 2 onboarding had already closed it — but the stakeholder reports kept showing the gap. The one-shot propagation bundle: (a) memory file + MEMORY.md index entry; (b) DevOps_Teams.md row 11; (c) Department Workflows V5 folder with updated QA-QC section + gap-closure note replacing the stale gap callout; (d) OKR Dashboard v8 with member count 18→19 + QA-QC team card 1 member→2 members + Team Pulse table row + 3 JS arrays (fetchMetrics prefix, member object array, teamInfoMap); (e) Project Management Handling_v4 with same QA-QC structural updates; (f) Access Upgrade v7 adding Afthab to Batch 1 with Basic+Test Plans license (same tier as Mostafa, because QC engineers require Test Plans management — NOT the default Basic tier) with cost update $142→$194/mo ($1,704→$2,328/yr); (g) this lesson. Key sub-lesson: when adding a second member to a role that already exists on the team, match the existing member's license tier and batch placement by default — don't slot the new hire into the cheapest tier just because they are a "new" line item. Otherwise the stakeholder report produces a misleading cost figure (would have been $148/mo, actual $194/mo — a 31% undercount). **Why:** Team rosters are the skeleton that every stakeholder-facing dashboard and report hangs off. Stale gap callouts in the rosters cause downstream reports to tell the wrong story (e.g. "QA-QC is still understaffed, needs urgent hire" when the hire is already onboarded and 3 weeks into KT). The cost of fragmented propagation is cumulative: every report generated between the gap-closure-in-reality and gap-closure-in-documentation carries the wrong message.
