Propagating all six files in a single batch — triggered by the single moment when a role is confirmed — prevents downstream drift for the rest of the quarter. **How to apply:** When a user confirms a team member's role for the first time (phrases like "his role is X", "she's a Y", "add him to the Z team"), run the 6-file propagation bundle in one batch: (1) save a canonical memory file and update MEMORY.md index; (2) update DevOps_Teams.md (the team directory); (3) create the next numbered version of Department Workflows and close any matching "Current Gap Identified" note in the relevant department section; (4) create the next numbered version of OKR & KPI Dashboard with roster count and team card updates (member count in header badge, team-card-header "N members" label, Team Pulse table row, 3 JS arrays: fetchMetrics `members`, memberDefs `name/key` objects, teamInfoMap `members/keys`); (5) create the next numbered version of Project Management Handling (same 5 update points as OKR); (6) create the next numbered version of Access Upgrade with user added to the appropriate batch + cost totals updated + V{N} changelog row added. ALSO: (7) ensure an empty role (former member, QA-QC single-member, backend 1-person team) gap flag that is now resolved is explicitly rewritten from "Current Gap Identified" to "Gap Closure ({Month} {Year})" — don't just delete the flag; stakeholders need the audit trail showing the gap was closed, not swept away. Surfaced by the Afthab P A (QC/Wallet) case on Apr 21, 2026.


## Heuristic Text Parsing & Data Pipeline Hygiene (Apr 21, 2026)

468. **Factual placeholder beats empty or invented when the source data is literally unrecoverable** — When a source artifact exists but cannot be read (e.g., image-only PDFs that fail text extraction with zero characters recovered, locked Outlook .msg files, corrupted attachments), the row/entry must still carry a label. The honest choice is a descriptive placeholder like `Unreadable CV (1776112376090.pdf)` — never a blank field, never a fabricated guess. **Why:** Two user-stated rules often collide — "don't leave any row without a name" AND "don't assume any name". A factual placeholder satisfies both: it's a real, verifiable string that tells the reader exactly what state the data is in. Invented values (blindly guessing a name from a filename stem) cross into fabrication; blank values violate the completeness guarantee and usually cascade into downstream joins and filters silently dropping rows. **How to apply:** (1) Detect unrecoverable cases at the extraction layer, not the rendering layer — mark them with a distinct confidence tag (e.g. `name_conf = "unparsed"`) so downstream consumers can treat them specially. (2) The placeholder string must include the source identifier (filename, URL, message ID) so the human reviewer can trace back. (3) Surface the unparsed count in the run summary — hiding them behind the placeholder is as bad as hiding them behind a blank. (4) Applies beyond CVs: any bulk ingest (emails, receipts, scanned invoices, OCR pipelines, satellite image metadata) with partial failure modes. Surfaced by the D:\HR candidate-name audit on Apr 21, 2026 — 3 of 51 fixed candidates had image-only PDFs with zero extracted text.

469. **Heuristic text extractors fail in three predictable classes — blocklist all three with complementary data structures** — On 2026-04-21, the candidate-name parser in `D:\HR\_pipeline\step2_parse_and_score.py` misread 51 names as (a) section headers like `Work History` / `Core Skills`, (b) education/credential phrases like `Bachelor of Computer Science` / `Future University in Egypt`, and (c) 2-word capitalized phrases that coincidentally look like names such as `Nasr City` / `Saudi Arabia`. The original heuristic had only ~25 section-header entries and missed all three classes. **Why:** Each class needs a different data structure. Exact-match sets catch single phrases ("work history"), substring-match sets catch variants ("bachelor of", "bachelor degree", "bachelor's degree" all reduce to a single "bachelor of" prefix), and per-word disqualifier sets catch any line containing a poisoned token. One list catches one class — trying to use a single exact-match set for all three was the root cause. **How to apply:** (1) Build three blocklist structures up front in any heuristic extractor: `SECTION_HEADERS` (exact match), `NON_NAME_SUBSTRINGS` / `NON_MATCH_SUBSTRINGS` (substring match for multi-word variants), and `DISQUALIFIERS` (per-word match). (2) Seed them from a real 10-20 case audit of actual production data — do not rely on imagination. (3) When a new false positive is reported, classify it first: is this an exact phrase, a multi-word variant, or a poisoned token? Add to the matching structure that fits — not to whichever list is most convenient. (4) Applies beyond names — the same pattern fits email subject-line classifiers, product-title parsers, and any regex-heuristic layer upstream of an ML model.

470. **Scan order matters in multi-pass parsers — specific/tight rules must run BEFORE broad/noisy rules** — In the extraction function `extract_name()`, running single-line separator-splitting on lines 1-50 BEFORE the "combine two consecutive short lines" pass caused regressions: a CV with "Ola" on line 1 and "Mohammed" on line 2 (each a single word needing consecutive-line combine) was instead parsed as "Nasr City" from line 9's address `Nasr City, Cairo, Egypt` — the broad rule matched first and short-circuited the specific one. Reordering — specific rule first, broad rule second — fixed this. **Why:** In heuristic parsers, the first passing rule wins. If a noisy/broad rule runs early, it will occasionally claim a false positive before the correct tight rule has a chance to fire. The intuitive ordering ("scan top-down, try every rule on every line") is wrong for rule pipelines — you want confidence-ordered, not location-ordered. **How to apply:** (1) List all heuristic passes with their precision/recall tradeoff. Tight rules (specific patterns, narrow scope) go first. Broad rules (fuzzy separators, wider scope) go last. (2) For each rule, enumerate what it *will* match that it shouldn't. If that list is non-empty and the next-tighter rule would catch those cases, reorder. (3) Run a diff harness after every rule-order change — the surface area for unintended matches is wide. (4) Applies to any ordered rule engine: spam filters, fraud-detection thresholds, CSS selector specificity, redirect rule lists, firewall rules.

471. **Scope fuzzy rules to the narrowest window where they pay off** — Separator-splitting on double-space `  `, `|`, `·`, `•`, `, ` is valuable on the CV header line ("Ahmed Abaza  Junior Quality Manager" → splits to `["Ahmed Abaza", "Junior Quality Manager"]`, first chunk is the name) but toxic on line 9 of the same CV ("Nasr City, Cairo, Egypt" → splits to `["Nasr City", "Cairo", "Egypt"]`, first chunk is a location). Same rule, different damage by scope. Limiting the separator-split pass to the first 5 lines (the header zone) eliminated all location false positives without losing the header wins. **Why:** Fuzzy rules carry both signal and noise; the signal-to-noise ratio depends on WHERE in the document they fire. Document structure gives you implicit scope information — headers are name-dense, body is fact-dense, footers are reference-dense. Applying a fuzzy rule uniformly across all regions ignores this and collects noise that cancels the signal. **How to apply:** (1) For every fuzzy/noisy rule, ask: "what is the narrowest span of the document where this rule pays off?" Default to lines 1-5 for CV headers, the first paragraph for news articles, the subject line for emails, the first screen for web pages. (2) Log where the rule fires during development — if >30% of fires are outside the intended zone, narrow the scope. (3) Never apply separator-splitting, letter-spaced recovery, or email-inference fallbacks globally — always scope them to the region they were designed for. (4) Applies to OCR post-processing, address extraction, author/byline detection, table-header detection, CSV-guessing heuristics.

472. **Arab/Egyptian compound names need hyphen-aware parsing — reject only lowercase-hyphenated phrases, never "any hyphen in a multi-word line"** — Names like `El-Absawy`, `Abd-Elhamid`, `Al-Zanaty`, `Abu-Bakr`, `Al-Hussein` must pass the name-plausibility check. The original rule "if line contains `-` AND has multiple words, reject" silently dropped every candidate with a hyphenated surname segment — forcing them into the email-fallback path and often producing incomplete or wrong names. The correct rule: "each hyphen-separated segment of a word must start with a capital letter". This accepts `El-Absawy` (both caps) and `Abu-Bakr` (both caps), but still rejects English compound phrases like `web-development`, `end-to-end`, `results-driven`, `object-oriented` (all lowercase). **Why:** In the MENA hiring pool, a material fraction of surnames contain an Arabic definite-article hyphen (`El-`, `Al-`, `Abd-`, `Abu-`, `Ibn-`). A blanket hyphen-reject rule discriminates against them by silently dropping their CVs into a fallback path with worse accuracy. The English lowercase-hyphen-phrase pattern is structurally different (lowercase-first, kebab-case) and can be distinguished with a per-segment capital check. **How to apply:** (1) In any name-validation routine, replace the "reject multi-word lines with hyphens" rule with a per-word check: for each word containing `-`, split on `-` and verify each segment's first character is uppercase. (2) Add explicit test cases for Arab compound names alongside the English-kebab rejection cases. (3) Applies to any global HR system, authorship metadata, directory listings, or ID verification that needs to handle multi-cultural name formats (also relevant for Spanish `de la`, Dutch `van der`, etc. — add those as supplementary rules).

473. **Regression-test the WHOLE corpus, not just the known-bad cases you set out to fix** — The 51 targeted name fixes in step2 silently introduced 4 regressions on previously-correct rows (Ola Mohammed, Amir El-Absawy, Software Quality Control, Saudi Arabia) because my before/after test harness only compared outcomes against the 51 known-bad fix targets. The 342 other candidates — whose names had already been parsing correctly — were outside the test loop, so a heuristic change that now mis-parses them went undetected until I scanned the final HTML output. Adding a "scan all output for suspicious patterns NOT in your fix list" gate caught the regressions and prompted the hyphen-rule and separator-scope fixes. **Why:** Targeted heuristic changes rarely stay targeted — any adjustment to a shared parser, filter, or scorer has blast radius across every row that touches the changed code path. Testing only the rows you *meant* to fix confirms you addressed the intended bug but gives zero signal on the rows you didn't mean to break. **How to apply:** (1) Before any heuristic change, snapshot the full current output (all rows, not just the bad ones). (2) After the change, diff the full new output against the snapshot. (3) Any row whose value changed AND that wasn't on your intended-fix list is a regression candidate — triage each one explicitly. (4) For production pipelines, automate this as a gate: fail the build if the diff touches rows outside the allowed changeset. (5) Applies to any rule/scorer/filter change — spam classifiers, fraud thresholds, tax-category mappers, pricing rules, permission matrices.

474. **Empty return beats wrong-but-plausible, when a fallback chain exists** — When a heuristic extractor returns `""` (empty), the next stage in the fallback chain (email-local-part inference, filename stem, structured-metadata lookup) can recover. When it returns "Nasr City" (a wrong-but-plausible 2-word phrase), the chain short-circuits because `""` is falsy but `"Nasr City"` is truthy — so the wrong answer ships. Tuning the primary heuristic toward restraint ("return empty if I'm not confident") is strictly better than tuning it toward coverage ("return something") for any pipeline that has downstream fallbacks. **Why:** Short-circuit chains (`result or fallback1 or fallback2 or default`) are common in Python, JavaScript, Ruby — they stop at the first truthy value. A falsy-but-honest empty is transparent to the chain; a truthy-but-wrong value is opaque and blocks recovery. The asymmetry is: a false negative (rejecting a real name) costs one fallback step; a false positive (returning a fake name) costs the entire correctness of that row. **How to apply:** (1) In any extractor feeding a fallback chain, bias toward `return None` / `return ""` over "best guess" when confidence is below a threshold. (2) Use an explicit confidence tag (`name_conf = "ok" | "email-derived" | "filename-fallback" | "unparsed"`) so downstream rendering can flag the provenance. (3) Log empty-return cases — they are not errors, they are correct honest outputs. (4) Do NOT apply this pattern when there is no fallback chain — a single-shot extractor must return its best guess or the row is lost. (5) Applies to OCR confidence thresholds, ML-classifier calibration (abstain-option), entity-resolution joins, and any parser/normalizer with a "don't know" option.

475. **Always keep a post-processor override layer for the long tail the heuristic can't reach** — Even a well-tuned heuristic name parser won't hit 100% on messy real-world CVs — graphic layouts that bury the name past contact/skills/certificates blocks, all-image PDFs, letter-spaced headers spanning 3 lines, multilingual headers with mixed scripts. After the Apr 21 step2 audit, the heuristic direct-hit rate for the 51 known-bad candidates went from 0/51 to 29/51 (57%) — a big win, but still leaves 22 cases needing help. The persistent solution is a small manual-override file (`D:\HR\_pipeline\fix_names.py`) mapping `filename → (corrected_name, name_conf)`, applied as the final pipeline step. It's idempotent, survives step2 re-runs, and lets the PM append new overrides in one line without touching the heuristic. **Why:** Heuristics have fundamental limits on messy natural-language data. Chasing 100% parser accuracy via more blocklists produces a parser that is overly conservative, breaks on legitimate new inputs, and still misses the truly hard cases. An override layer cleanly separates "rules that generalize" (in the parser) from "exceptions that don't" (in the override file), keeping each code path simple. **How to apply:** (1) Every extract/parse/transform pipeline with human-review output should have an override step as its last stage. (2) The override format should be plain data (dict, JSON, YAML, CSV) that a non-coder can edit — `{filename: name}`, `{id: status}`, `{key: value}` — not a script. (3) The override layer applies unconditionally; it does NOT check whether the upstream stage disagreed — that conflict is the whole point of the override. (4) Document the override file in the pipeline's README as "the tail-end corrector" — otherwise future maintainers will delete it thinking it's dead code. (5) Applies to: ETL pipelines, ML-inference post-processing, address geocoders, product-catalog enrichment, PII redaction. Pair with Lesson 44 (save lessons) and Lesson 286 (don't assume source data) — the override file becomes part of the source data for the next pipeline run.


## Cross-File Hyperlink Audit & MD/HTML Parity (Apr 21, 2026 — continued)

468. **When creating a new numbered version of an HTML file, audit it for hard-coded references to the OLD version number across ALL attribute types — not just `<a href>`** — On 2026-04-21, after copying `Project Management Handling_v3.html` → `_v4.html` and updating the QA-QC team data, I declared the file ready. User asked "did you consider the relation between them if have hyperlink or something like that?" — prompting a re-audit that surfaced **5 stale references to `OKR_KPI_Dashboard_v7.html`** buried inside `data-hidden-reason` attributes on hidden tab buttons (lines 387, 398, 404, 413, 416). These informational strings telling users "OKR Progress tab belongs to OKR_KPI_Dashboard_v7.html — the canonical OKR tracker" needed to point to v8 now that v8 existed. Similar stale pattern found in V5/00_Index.html (title "V4 Index", metadata version 4.0, date Feb 15). The visible `<a href>` audit had passed cleanly — all 30+ `href` values were Azure DevOps portal URLs (version-independent). The miss was in non-href attributes: `data-*`, `<title>`, header metadata, changelog rows, inline comments referencing sibling-file names. **Why:** When versioning a file, the mental model "update the links" only covers `<a href>` and `<link rel>`. But real cross-references in modern HTMLs live in 6+ places: `<title>` tags, `<meta>` descriptions, `data-hidden-reason` / `data-tooltip` / `data-*` attributes, JS string constants, version-history tables, footer text, localStorage key names. A file that passes the href-audit can still ship with 5 stale attribute references that tell the user "see v7" when v7 is no longer canonical — degrading the document's authority. **How to apply:** After copying file-vN → file-v(N+1), run a single regex grep for the OLD version number across the whole file — `grep -n "_v{N}\|V{N}\.html\|Version.*{N}\.0\|{N} Index"` — BEFORE declaring the new version ready. Every match is a potential stale reference. Additionally audit: (a) `<title>` tag content; (b) `<meta>` description/keywords; (c) all `data-*` attribute values that look like prose; (d) JS string literals that name sibling files; (e) version-history table rows (keep old rows AS-IS, add new row at top/bottom); (f) footer/header text announcing "vN"; (g) any `localStorage` key names that embed the version. The grep takes 2 seconds; the miss costs credibility when the user spots it mid-review. Pair with lesson #467 — cross-file audits are part of the retroactive propagation discipline.

469. **MD/HTML parity check is not optional — grep sibling MDs before declaring an HTML-only update complete** — On 2026-04-21, after delivering 7 HTML files with Afthab's role change (OKR v8, PM Handling v4, Access Upgrade v7, V5 Dept Workflows suite), I announced completion. User asked "did md file for this html files is updated with what we do here?" — prompting an MD audit that found **2 authoritative MD files with stale team rosters**: `DevOps_Complete_Setup_Guide_V1.md` (line 80: Mostafa as only QC; line 310: Phase 3 "Upgrade Mostafa Ahmed to Basic + Test Plans") and `DevOps_Access_Level_Recommendation_Full_V3.md` (line 83: Mostafa as row 9, sole QA-QC entry). Both MDs are indexed by the local ragtools knowledge base — so any future search for "QA-QC team members" or "Azure DevOps access plan" would return stale answers long after the HTML had been fixed. Plus, the user's standing convention "HTML-only after vN" for OKR and Access Upgrade email-summary series had been interpreted by me as "no MD parity needed" — but the convention applied only to those specific versioned series, NOT to the authoritative complete-setup-guide + full-recommendation reference docs in the same folder. **Why:** MDs are the authoritative reference layer for the RAG index. HTMLs are the presentation layer for stakeholders. When team/cost/role data changes, BOTH layers need the update — the HTML so stakeholders see it, the MD so the RAG index returns the right answer. "HTML-only after vN" is a rule for *versioned releases of the same document*, not a license to skip MDs that carry overlapping team data. The deeper failure mode: Search the knowledge base BEFORE declaring a propagation task done — if RAG still returns stale answers after you've updated the HTML, the work isn't complete. **How to apply:** For every HTML update that touches team rosters, cost tables, org structure, or access lists: (1) `ls *.md` in the same folder and grep each for the entity being changed (person name, role, cost line); (2) audit sibling folders that commonly carry overlapping data — `DevOps Access Level` has both email-summary MDs AND complete-setup-guide MDs AND recommendation-full MDs; (3) if the MD is a versioned-series that follows "HTML-only after vN" (e.g. OKR v3+ and Access Upgrade v4+ per user's convention), restore parity by creating a fresh MD mirroring the latest HTML content — it takes 10 minutes and protects the RAG index; (4) if the MD is an authoritative legacy reference (Complete_Setup_Guide_V1, Access_Level_Recommendation_Full_V3), patch it minimally with a prominent "⚠️ Legacy reference — minimally patched, not fully restated" banner plus the specific roster/Phase/checklist updates AND a pointer to the current canonical HTML; (5) DO NOT recompute cost accounting in legacy MDs without a full rewrite — just flag that cost figures are stale. Pair with lessons #436 (search local project data files before saying "not found") and #467 (retroactive propagation) — #436 says to search files, #467 says to propagate across registries, #469 says specifically: check BOTH MD and HTML layers of the same data, because the RAG index reads MDs.

481. **Before editing on a "file has stale content" report, hash the file on disk and grep the actual references** — On 2026-04-21 a user report said `Project Management Handling_v4.html` linked to `OKR_KPI_Dashboard_v7.html`. Direct verification via `sha256sum` + `grep -n -o 'href="file:///.../OKR_KPI_Dashboard_v[0-9]+\.html"'` on the live file took 10 seconds and proved the on-disk file was correct — all 6 references pointed to v8. The real cause was a browser-cache hit rendering a stale prior version. Had I taken the report at face value, I would have re-edited an already-correct file and introduced inconsistency. **Why:** User reports of "file X says Y" are observations of a rendered view (browser tab, editor preview, HTML export) — not of what the file on disk actually contains. Browser caches, CDN caches, editor snapshot buffers, and stale file-watchers routinely lie. Trusting the report and editing the file can flip a correct file to wrong. **How to apply:** (1) For any "the file says X, should be Y" report, first verify on-disk state: mtime + size + SHA-256 + targeted `grep -n -o` for the specific pattern. (2) If the on-disk state already matches what the user expects, the issue is in the view layer — direct them to hard-refresh (Ctrl+F5), clear cache, or re-open the file. (3) Only edit when on-disk grep confirms the stale value. (4) In the reply, show the evidence (hash, mtime, line-numbered grep output) — proves the point and teaches the diagnostic pattern. Pair with Lesson #273 (HTML-comment rot) and the general principle: the file on disk is authoritative; the render is not.


## MD Merge & Consolidation Workflow (Apr 21, 2026)

476. **Appendix heading prefixes prevent anchor collisions in multi-source MD merges** — When merging multiple MD files verbatim into one document (e.g., `v1.md` + `v2.md` → consolidated `.md`), identical section names across sources ("Objective 1", "Team Roster") collide in the Markdown TOC because both resolve to the same auto-generated anchor slug. Fix: shift original headings down one level AND add a unique per-source prefix such as `A1 Objective 1` / `A2 Objective 1`. Surfaced on 2026-04-21 merging `OKR_KPI_Dashboard_v1.md` + `_v2.md` + `_v8.md` into `OKR & KPI.md` — both v1 and v2 had `## Objective 1` and `## Team Roster`, which without prefixes would produce two `#objective-1` anchors silently clobbering each other. **Why:** Markdown renderers generate anchors from heading slug (`## Team Roster` → `#team-roster`). Two identical H2s in the same document both resolve to the same anchor; the TOC link and any in-document hyperlink jumps only to the first match. Prefixes keep each appendix's navigation independent and audit-ready. **How to apply:** (1) Before merging, pre-scan all source files for duplicate H2/H3 text — `grep -h "^## " *.md | sort | uniq -d`. (2) For every duplicate, demote all copies down one level inside the appendix (so the main doc's `##` section headers still own the top-level TOC) AND add a short per-source prefix (`A1 …` / `A2 …` / `Legacy …`) to each appendix's sub-headings. (3) Apply prefixes only in appendices/supplement content — the primary skeleton (per Lesson #115) keeps its natural headings. (4) Applies to any multi-source consolidation: merged MOM documents, combined quarterly reports, audit-trail archives, multi-version technical specs.

477. **For "merge into one file" tasks, the PM default is full preservation + delete originals** — When asked to consolidate multiple MD/HTML versions into a single target file, two choices compound: **history depth** (full/condensed/current-only) and **source-file fate** (keep/delete/archive). The PM's default workflow is **full preservation** (keep all source tables verbatim in appendices) paired with **delete originals after merge** — the merged file becomes the single audit trail. Confirmed on 2026-04-21 by the OKR & KPI merge (v1 + v2 + v8 → `OKR & KPI.md`). **Why:** Full preservation protects against future "what did v1 actually say" questions; deleting originals prevents fragmented file sprawl and the risk of stakeholders reading outdated v1/v2 as if they were current. The two choices only work together — deleting without preservation loses data, and preservation without deletion defeats the "one file" goal. **How to apply:** (1) Before merging, if the consolidation scope is ambiguous, surface both questions via AskUserQuestion rather than guessing. (2) If the defaults apply, still announce both choices before execution ("I'll preserve v1+v2 as appendices and delete the originals after verification"). (3) Verify the merged file BEFORE any deletion runs: file size ≥ sum of source bodies minus shared boilerplate; grep for unique strings from each source ("Objective 5" → Appendix 1, "Tier 1 — Tracked in Detail" → Appendix 2); confirm all top-level section anchors exist. (4) Only delete after ALL verification checks pass — if any fail, keep the originals and ask the user to inspect. Pair with Lesson #138 (never bash `rm` on Windows user files — for low-risk derivative files after a merge, verified deletion is fine; for originals with irreplaceable content, use Recycle Bin) and Lesson #140 (originals are sacred — once merged, source MDs become derivatives eligible for deletion because the merged file carries 100% of their content).

478. **Plan-mode clarifiers with AskUserQuestion beat guessing on ambiguous consolidation tasks** — Two targeted questions (history depth, source-file fate) turned an ambiguous "merge all this md in one only" request into a deterministic spec. Without them, the output would likely have defaulted to condensed history or left source files in place — either of which would have required a rework cycle. Proven on 2026-04-21 during the OKR & KPI 4-file merge. **Why:** Merge/consolidation requests are structurally ambiguous because the user's mental model rarely distinguishes "consolidate content" from "consolidate files" from "preserve all history" from "keep just current state." Silent guessing produces outputs the user will reject; asking costs 10 seconds and locks the spec before writing a single line. **How to apply:** (1) In plan mode, on any task touching multiple sources/versions, use AskUserQuestion with 2-3 options per question BEFORE finalizing the plan. (2) Put the recommended option first and label it "(Recommended)". (3) Default question set for any merge: (a) history depth — full preservation / condensed / current-only; (b) source-file fate — keep / delete / archive. (4) Skip the clarifiers only if the user pre-specified the choice, or the task is truly trivial (single-line typo fix, one-file rename). (5) Applies beyond merges to any structurally ambiguous request: renaming a folder (move/copy), removing content from a deliverable (delete-from-file/remove-from-publish-only), updating a shared template (in-place/new-version), handing off a project (read-only archive/live-editable).


## Plugin Design — Skills-First vs Hooks-First (Apr 21, 2026)

483. **For Claude Code plugins, authoring-time skills beat post-write hooks for most coverage gaps** — When extending `pm-guidelines-plugin` from v1.4 → v1.7 to close 12 coverage gaps from global_lessons.md, the initial plan leaned heavily on hook extensions (regex checks in `post_write_dispatcher.py`). User course-corrected: "decrease the creation of hooks and use the skills instead as much as possible." The revised plan delivered 3 new skills + 2 new agents + 6 skill extensions + 0 new hooks — closing all 12 gaps with visible authoring-time guidance instead of silent post-write enforcement. **Why:** Hooks (Python scripts on PostToolUse) fire AFTER the mistake lands in the file. They're silent until a regex matches; when they fail they fail quietly; they're Python which carries a cross-platform maintenance tax. Skills (Markdown with prompt-signal activation) fire BEFORE the mistake — at the moment Claude is about to write the wrong thing. They're visible when they activate; they're iterable without restarting the harness; they're domain-portable. The right division of labor: hooks for deterministic security blocks (PAT tokens, protected files), dispatcher for regex-detectable quality checks, skills for everything else (workflows, checklists, judgment-heavy rules, cross-file audits delegated to read-only agents). **How to apply:** (1) When adding new coverage to a plugin, default to a new skill or skill-extension, not a new hook. (2) New hooks are only justified when: the check is regex-detectable AND must fire unprompted (e.g., security gate) AND fits the existing dispatcher's scope. (3) For judgment-heavy or cross-file work, pair an authoring-time skill (prevents) with a read-only audit agent (verifies) — see Lesson #485. (4) Measure coverage via the plugin's own health check after every change; the description-string carries a "N guidelines" pattern that the lesson-count validator expects — keep it current. (5) Applies to any Claude Code plugin, not just pm-guidelines: see `d:\Global Lessons\plans\soft-tinkering-taco.md` for the worked routing table across 12 gaps.

484. **Plan-mode agent forks with different architectural theses produce better plans than a single agent** — When designing the v1.5+ upgrade, I launched TWO Plan agents in parallel with opposing instructions: Agent A was told to maximize new skills/agents; Agent B was told to minimize new files and extend existing surfaces. The divergence surfaced real trade-offs: Agent B correctly identified that `pm-cross-tab-reconciler` naturally lives in `pm-report-reviewer`'s rubric; Agent A correctly identified that forcing everything into one agent overloads it. Agent B also caught a path error (Agent A used `d:\Global Lessons\pm-guidelines-plugin\`; Agent B verified on disk and found the correct `d:\Global Lessons\plugins\pm-guidelines-plugin\`). The final synthesis absorbed the cheap extensions from B's thesis AND the specialist agents from A's thesis — better than either alone. **Why:** A single Plan agent optimizes for its given direction. Two agents with opposing directions surface the actual design space — where one agent's optimum is clearly wrong, the other's is clearly right; where they agree, you have high-confidence structure; where they disagree, you have a genuine architectural fork to present to the user via AskUserQuestion. Cheap (two agents running in parallel is ~same wall time as one) and higher-quality. **How to apply:** (1) For any non-trivial plan involving architectural choices, launch 2-3 Plan agents with EXPLICITLY DIFFERENT theses — "minimize X", "maximize Y", "prefer Z". (2) Brief each agent on the OTHER agent's thesis so they don't accidentally converge. (3) Synthesize by cross-checking divergent recommendations against ground truth (file-system verification, memory entries, CLAUDE.md rules). (4) When both agents arrive at the same recommendation, trust it. When they diverge, offer the user the fork via AskUserQuestion rather than picking silently. (5) Applies to: plugin design, database migration strategy, API redesign, codebase refactoring plans, any task with real architectural trade-offs.

485. **The skill-plus-agent dual-surface pattern is the right shape for judgment-heavy coverage** — Gaps like "dashboard cross-tab number drift" and "MD↔HTML parity" aren't solvable by a single surface. At authoring time, you need a skill that teaches Claude the single-source-of-truth rule BEFORE writing code. At audit time, you need a read-only agent that semantically aligns near-synonym labels ("Open Items" ≈ "Outstanding Tasks") using Opus judgment across the finished file. Using the SAME NAME for the skill and the agent (e.g., `pm-cross-tab-reconciler` skill + `pm-cross-tab-reconciler` agent) reinforces that they solve the same problem at different times — skill prevents, agent verifies. Proven in v1.5+v1.6 of pm-guidelines plugin: cross-tab-reconciler shipped as both skill (Phase 1) and agent (Phase 2); md-html-parity-checker agent (Phase 2) pairs with the `pm-session-discipline` rule "MD is source of truth" (Phase 1). **Why:** Skills activate on prompt signals — they fire while Claude is authoring. Agents are invoked explicitly or via delegation — they fire after authoring is done. Neither alone covers the full lifecycle: a skill with no agent means mistakes that slip past authoring are never caught; an agent with no skill means Claude repeats the mistake every session. The same-name convention makes the pairing visible in the plugin structure (`pm-X/SKILL.md` + `agents/pm-X.md`). **How to apply:** (1) For judgment-heavy gaps, design BOTH surfaces from day one: skill for authoring, agent for audit. (2) Use the same name for both when they solve the same problem; Claude disambiguates via context (prompt signal → skill; explicit launch or reviewer delegation → agent). (3) Cross-reference in both files: skill's body says "if unsure, invoke the agent for post-authoring semantic review"; agent's body says "this is the audit side of the skill — when the skill's discipline is followed, this agent should report PARITY CLEAR". (4) Wire the main reviewer agent to auto-delegate to specialist agents when the target type matches — avoids overloading one agent with every possible check. (5) Applies to any domain with both authoring and audit phases: CI pipeline rules, document quality standards, security review, accessibility compliance.


## Folder Absorption & Multi-Source Consolidation (Apr 22, 2026)

486. **Additive-over-destructive is the data-loss safety net for folder absorptions** — When absorbing a sibling folder into a parent (e.g., `QA-QC & Devlopment Activity - RACI Matrix\` and `Taqat Alignment Framework - Hatem\` into `Department Workflows\` on 2026-04-22), never delete or overwrite the originals during the merge. Move the folder as-is, copy the content into the aggregator MD/HTML, and keep every source file at its new path with its original mtime. The aggregator becomes the primary reading surface; the originals stay as the rollback. **Why:** A merge that deletes originals has no rollback if the aggregator turns out to be missing content, mis-demoting headings, or flattening tables. "Don't lose data" isn't just about the merge pass — it's about the hours, weeks, and future sessions after the merge when someone needs to cross-check a specific claim against the raw source. Additive consolidation costs a few MB of disk; destructive consolidation can cost a weekend of forensic reconstruction. **How to apply:** (1) Default rule: absorb-by-move, never absorb-by-delete — the moved folder keeps every file intact at its new path. (2) Aggregator file (typically a consolidated `Department Workflows.md` or similar) adds content, never replaces. (3) A consolidation is only "complete" when: originals exist at their new path, aggregator has provenance markers (Lesson #487), subfolder stubs carry relocation breadcrumbs (Lesson #489), and the index registry is updated. (4) Only consider deleting originals after the user explicitly asks for cleanup — and even then, prefer Recycle Bin over `rm`. (5) Pair with Lesson #477 (full preservation is the PM default) — but recognize that #477 allows delete-after-verification for versioned MD series with a single consolidated target, whereas folder absorption keeps the originals because they represent independent inbound work.

487. **Every merged block needs a `<!-- SOURCE: path, mtime, size-metric -->` provenance marker** — When concatenating many source files into one aggregator, prefix every included block with a single HTML-comment marker: `<!-- SOURCE: V5\md\01_Organization_and_Governance.md, 2026-04-21 15:51:12, 172 lines -->`. This makes it trivial to (a) prove which block came from which source, (b) detect duplicate inclusions, (c) audit completeness via `grep -c "^<!-- SOURCE:"`, (d) spot-check drift if a source is later edited (compare current mtime to the marker). Applied on 2026-04-22 during the Department Workflows consolidation — 24 markers for 2 DOCX + 2 HTML + 20 MD sources, each verifiable in one grep. **Why:** Without markers, a merged file is a black box — you can't tell which section came from which source, whether anything was silently dropped, or whether a future re-run would produce the same output. With markers, the aggregator is self-auditing: the marker count should equal the source count, and `grep "^<!-- SOURCE:" | sort | uniq -c` catches duplicates. **How to apply:** (1) In any merge script that concatenates files, emit a SOURCE marker before each block — path relative to the aggregator's folder, source mtime as ISO timestamp, one size-metric (lines for MD, paragraphs/tables for DOCX, headings/paragraphs/lists/tables for HTML). (2) After the merge, run `grep -c "^<!-- SOURCE:"` and assert it equals the expected source count — build this as an automated check, not a mental exercise. (3) When citing a block in a later discussion or quality check, quote the marker alongside the content so reviewers can trace back. (4) Don't count `<!-- SOURCE:` inside regular prose — only match on `^<!-- SOURCE:` (line-anchored) to avoid false positives when the aggregator mentions the marker pattern in documentation. (5) Applies to any multi-source concatenation: merged MD reports, combined HTML dashboards, stitched transcript files, audit-log bundles.

488. **DOCX tables require structured XML walking, not regex-over-paragraphs** — First pass of the 2026-04-22 QA-QC RACI DOCX extraction flattened every table cell to a one-cell-per-paragraph list (each `<w:tc>` cell wraps a `<w:p>` paragraph internally, so splitting on `</w:p>` and stripping tags yields "Activity / BO / PM / DEV / QA / QC / Define Business Requirements / R / C / C / C / I / …" — a vertical column of orphaned cells with the grid structure destroyed). Fixed by walking the body XML linearly, branching into `<w:tbl>` → `<w:tr>` → `<w:tc>` → `<w:p>` and emitting real Markdown tables. The 6-phase RACI (26 activities × 5 roles) rendered correctly on the second pass. **Why:** Word's OOXML format stores table cells as paragraphs-inside-cells-inside-rows — a regex-over-paragraphs approach sees only leaf content, losing the hierarchical structure. A linear XML walk preserves the nesting. Markdown tables can express everything Word RACI tables express, but only if you first parse the tree. **How to apply:** (1) Any DOCX-to-Markdown transcription that touches tables needs an explicit table branch — not just paragraph extraction. (2) Walk the `<w:body>` linearly with `re.finditer(r'<w:(p|tbl)\b[^>]*>', ..., re.DOTALL)` tracking element boundaries; emit `('para', text)` for paragraphs and `('table', rows)` for tables in document order so paragraph-before-table and paragraph-after-table ordering is preserved. (3) For each table, walk `<w:tr>` then `<w:tc>` then join the `<w:t>` runs inside each cell's `<w:p>` elements — this gets the cell text cleanly. (4) Normalize row length (pad short rows with empty cells), escape pipes in cells (`|` → `\|`), emit the header row, separator, and body rows as standard Markdown. (5) Same principle applies to HTML extraction — walk `<h1-h6>, <p>, <li>, <table>` in document order with a single block-level regex, don't concatenate all `<td>` text as flat paragraphs. (6) If tables aren't critical for the use case, state that upfront and fall back to paragraph-only extraction — but never silently flatten a table and pretend it was prose.

489. **Folder relocation needs a three-layer breadcrumb: subfolder stub → aggregator front matter → Source-Root index** — When moving a folder under a new parent AND consolidating its content into an aggregator MD, three separate pointers must be planted so the relocation is findable from any entry point: (a) **Subfolder stub** — the stub MD inside the moved folder (e.g., `QA-QC & Devlopment Activity - RACI Matrix.md`) gets a "⚠ RELOCATED" banner with old path, new path, date, reason, and the aggregator Part number where content lives. (b) **Aggregator front matter** — the parent's consolidated MD (e.g., `Department Workflows.md`) gets a "Consolidation / Move History" table listing every absorbed source by date, from/to paths, and landing Part. (c) **Source-Root index** — the old session entry (e.g., section 2.6 Taqat Alignment) is marked `*(ABSORBED YYYY-MM-DD into 2.N)*` with old+new paths preserved, not deleted. Proven on 2026-04-22 across three absorptions: governance DOCX + QA-QC RACI folder + Taqat Alignment folder, all consolidated into `Department Workflows\`. **Why:** A user (or future-you) discovering a file may enter through any of three doors: opening the folder directly (sees the stub), reading the aggregator (sees the front matter table), or searching the session index (sees the absorbed marker). Plant the breadcrumb in only one layer and the other two entry points will return stale answers — the stub will look like a current session when it's really a relocated artifact, the aggregator will look like it was built from scratch when it really absorbed existing work, and the index will either still point to the old (non-existent) path or silently delete the session and lose the audit trail. **How to apply:** (1) For every folder move + consolidation, plan the three breadcrumbs as a single atomic unit — if you can only do one, delay the move until you can do all three. (2) Subfolder stub template: `> ⚠ RELOCATED <date> — Old path: X | New path: Y | Consolidated into: Z Part N | Originals preserved`. Keep the old H1 title so grep and RAG still find it. (3) Aggregator front matter: a table with columns Date / Action (Moved file | Absorbed folder) / From / To / Lands in (Part N). (4) Source-Root index: change the section heading to `### 2.N Original Name *(ABSORBED <date> into 2.M)*`, keep the original path labelled "Old Path (moved)" and add the new path underneath — never delete the entry. (5) Cross-check after planting: from each entry point, can you navigate to the other two? If no, a breadcrumb is missing. (6) Applies to any relocation that combines move + merge: folder consolidation, session absorption, project re-org, MD series collapse, vault restructuring.


## Legacy Reverse Engineering & Migration Planning (Apr 22, 2026)

490. **Private GitHub repo access — verify BEFORE cloning, not during** — When the user hands you a GitHub URL under a different owner's org (e.g. `mbashaPPQ/TTElectronicServices` while you're authenticated as `melshahedpearl`), run `gh auth status` **and** `gh repo view OWNER/REPO --json visibility,defaultBranchRef,pushedAt,description,diskUsage` BEFORE the clone. That one API call proves the token has read access, returns visibility (private/public), last-push date, and disk size — everything you need to decide whether the clone will succeed and how long it will take. **Why:** `gh repo view` returns JSON in ~1 second. A failed `git clone` with wrong credentials takes 30–300 seconds of hanging prompt + credential-manager popups + eventual rage-quit. On 2026-04-22 the `gh view` step caught that the repo was private, had one commit, and was ~19 MB — all answers relevant to the plan. **How to apply:** (1) When a GitHub URL comes from a different org/user than your authenticated identity, always `gh repo view` first. (2) If the view call 404s unauthenticated, you must either have the repo owner add your account OR clone via a token-authenticated URL. (3) Capture `diskUsage`, `pushedAt`, `defaultBranchRef.name` into the plan up front — saves a round-trip later. (4) Same check applies to Azure DevOps repos (`az repos show`), GitLab (`glab repo view`), Bitbucket — never skip the metadata probe. Reference: LEGACY_RE_BRIEF_ES.md Step 1 verification.

491. **Parallel Explore agents for wide codebase surveys — partition scope explicitly to prevent overlap** — On a 2,577-file C#/ASP.NET legacy solution (2026-04-22 TTElectronicServices reverse-engineering), launching 3 Explore agents in a single message (project-meta extraction / Tier-1 deep walks of 4 most-used pages / Tier-2 categorized index of remaining 52 pages) completed in the time the single-agent sequential version would have cost for just one tier. **Why:** Agents don't coordinate with each other — you coordinate them via mutually-exclusive prompt boundaries. The Tier-2 agent prompt explicitly said "exclude these 4 pages" and listed them; without that, it would have re-walked the Tier-1 files and duplicated work. **How to apply:** (1) For any codebase survey spanning ≥3 independent slices (per-module, per-layer, per-concern), launch agents in parallel with NON-OVERLAPPING scopes — enumerate the files/folders each agent owns vs excludes. (2) Max 3 agents per message (more becomes hard to audit). (3) Each prompt must be self-contained: agents can't read your earlier tool results — paste counts, file lists, and prior-agent findings into the prompt. (4) After parallel return, YOU synthesize — don't chain another agent on top to merge; do it yourself so you can spot cross-agent contradictions. (5) Save token+time budget by specifying thoroughness per agent ("very thorough" only where it matters; "medium" or "quick" for enumeration tasks). Reference: LEGACY_RE_BRIEF_ES.md Step 1 three-agent parallel scan.

492. **Always deep-read Excel and .msg reference files BEFORE writing the plan** — On 2026-04-22 the user's prompt "read and take action" was ambiguous. Reading the `.msg` via Outlook COM (`$outlook.CreateItemFromTemplate(path); $msg.Subject; $msg.Body`) revealed the strategic request was about TWO systems (Archiving + Electronic Services), not just the ES folder the session opened in, and that the central question was "Odoo vs Django/Next rebuild" — which anchored the entire plan's Section 10 decision matrix. Reading the `.xlsx` via Excel COM (`$excel.Workbooks.Open($path, $false, $true); $sheet.UsedRange`) surfaced the `AppearInMenu=True` column flagging the 4 most-used pages, which became the Tier-1 scope anchor. **Why:** An ambiguous prompt answered from only the folder structure would have produced the wrong scope (both repos), the wrong emphasis (inventory everything vs focus on 4 menu pages), and the wrong recommendation framing (generic vs Odoo-vs-Django). Cost of reading the 2 reference files: ~90 seconds. Cost of skipping: a wasted brief. **How to apply:** (1) When the working directory contains .xlsx, .msg, .pdf, .docx files alongside code/data, ALWAYS read them before writing the plan — never after. (2) Outlook COM pattern: `New-Object -ComObject Outlook.Application; $outlook.CreateItemFromTemplate(<path>); print Subject/From/To/CC/SentOn/Body`. (3) Excel COM pattern: `New-Object -ComObject Excel.Application; $excel.Visible = $false; Workbooks.Open(path, $false, $true); iterate Sheets and UsedRange cells`. (4) If output exceeds 30KB, persist to a tool-results file and Read it back. (5) Build the plan's Context section by quoting the .msg sender, date, and strategic ask verbatim — that anchors stakeholder alignment. Reference: LEGACY_RE_BRIEF_ES.md Context section.

493. **Legacy-RE deliverable: Decision Matrix > Prose, every time** — When the stakeholder's strategic question is "Option A vs Option B for N modules" (2026-04-22 Semir's Odoo-vs-Django ask spanning 9 ES modules), end the brief with a per-module scorecard table: columns `Module | Pages | Out-of-box fit | Stack A effort (S/M/L/XL) | Stack B effort | Recommendation`. Follow it with a recommended path (Hybrid/A-heavy/B-heavy) and exactly 3 critical caveats. **Why:** Decision-makers read the matrix first and everything else second. Prose-only briefs force them to build the matrix mentally — error-prone and time-consuming. The matrix+path+caveats trio is what stakeholders quote back in meetings and paste into follow-up emails. On 2026-04-22, Section 10's matrix (9 module rows × 6 columns) produced a Hybrid recommendation in ~25 weeks with 3 caveats (WCF coupling, Odoo bench strength, monolith decomposition first) — clean one-glance answer. **How to apply:** (1) Effort column uses consistent buckets: S ≤1 wk / M 2-4 wk / L 1-2 mo / XL >2 mo. (2) Out-of-box fit uses Full / Partial / None badges with color. (3) Every row ends with a Recommendation cell — don't let reviewer infer. (4) Below the table: named path (Hybrid/A-heavy/B-heavy) + 2-sentence paragraph on why it beats each pure alternative + exactly 3 caveats with specific remediation per caveat. (5) Mirror the matrix in both the MD and the HTML (with the same column order and badges) — don't strip it down for HTML. Reference: LEGACY_RE_BRIEF_ES.md Section 10.

494. **Monolith decomposition is always Phase 0, never Phase 1 or later** — When a legacy codebase has a page/module >2,000 LoC (2026-04-22 `RequestService.aspx.cs` = 2,936 LoC, `ServicesInbox.aspx.cs` = 1,084, `BlueprintDesigner.aspx.cs` = 843), the migration effort estimate depends heavily on whether the monolith is decomposed on the legacy platform FIRST. Going direct to rewrite bakes in worst-case assumptions and blows up effort on BOTH target stacks from L to XL. **Why:** Migrating a 2,936-LoC monolith whole-cloth forces the target stack to absorb the tangled coupling (dynamic form rendering + workflow orchestration + blueprint binding all in one class). Decomposing first (3 weeks on the legacy platform: extract pure logic classes, thin the UI controller, add characterization tests) reveals the real surface area — often 4-5 cohesive units, each L or less to migrate. Skipping decomposition makes Odoo XL and Django XL instead of L each. **How to apply:** (1) Add a "Phase 0 — Pre-work (3 wk)" before any rewrite phases: decompose monoliths + SP inventory + characterization tests + decision confirmation. (2) The "monolith" threshold is 2,000 LoC per class or 500 LoC per page or 200 LoC per React component — adjust per stack but flag aggressively. (3) Characterization tests around the decomposition prevent regression during the refactor AND become the golden-path regression suite for the migrated stack. (4) Applies to every stack: C# WebForms, Django fat views, Rails god-models, React god-components, Angular mega-services. (5) If the user pushes back on Phase 0 duration, show them the L→XL cost delta on the decision matrix — it's usually 4-6 weeks per target stack, dwarfing the 3-week Phase 0. Reference: LEGACY_RE_BRIEF_ES.md Section 12 Phase 0.

495. **One-commit GitHub repo named "Initial commit: X solution" = code dump, not the live repo** — On 2026-04-22 `mbashaPPQ/TTElectronicServices` showed exactly 1 commit dated the morning of the handoff email. That is a snapshot for the reverse-engineering exercise, NOT the production repo. **Why:** Contributor history, blame-traceable bug origins, feature-add timelines, PR patterns — none of those signals exist in a one-shot dump. Reviewers who don't read this signal may mistake "one contributor, one commit" for "immature project" or ask why there's no PR history. The real dev lifecycle lives in whatever TFS/internal-GitLab/Azure-DevOps the owner actually uses. **How to apply:** (1) In the repo-metadata inventory MD, always include a "What this repo does NOT contain" section listing: real commit history, SP source, tests, CI, DB schema, ENV template, README. (2) Explicitly say: "one-commit code dump, not the production repo" — don't let readers infer. (3) In the brief's migration-risk table, add a Medium-severity row for "Stored proc source not in-repo" + "Real repo history not available" with mitigation = request from owner. (4) Count files via `git ls-files | awk -F. '{print tolower($NF)}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn` to compare language-breakdown numbers with the GitHub-API language report — divergence tells you what binaries/generated files skew the web display. (5) If the repo is clean-room, also check for leftover developer scaffolding (the 2026-04-22 ES dump had `WebApplication1/` — a stray ASP.NET template project). Flag and recommend removal. Reference: `_inventory/github-metadata.md`.

496. **Stored-procedure source is almost never in the application repo — flag as "request from owner" upfront** — When a .NET codebase calls `Microsoft.ApplicationBlocks.Data.SqlHelper.ExecuteDataset` / `sp_*` patterns / EF `Database.SqlQuery`, assume the DDL and SP scripts are NOT in-repo until proven otherwise. They typically live in a sibling DB project (SSDT), a Redgate SQL Source Control repo, a SQL Server Management Studio backup, or DBA-owned scripts outside source control entirely. **Why:** On 2026-04-22 the 365 `.cs` files under `Library/DataAccess/` included SP calls via `ApplicationBlocks.Data` but the corresponding CREATE PROCEDURE source was absent — because it was in a separate DBA-owned SQL project. A migration estimate that ignores the SP layer misses 20-40% of the real effort (SP translation to ORM queries + testing + data-migration-dependent procs). **How to apply:** (1) During tech-stack metadata scan, grep for `sp_`, `usp_`, `SqlHelper.Execute`, `Database.SqlQuery`, `CommandType.StoredProcedure` — any hit means check for SP source in a sibling folder/project. (2) If not found, add a High-severity migration-risk row: "SP source NOT in-repo — request catalog from owner." (3) Ask for: (a) SP script files, (b) a SP-usage audit (which procs are live vs dead), (c) DDL for referenced tables. (4) Don't finalize migration-effort estimates until the SP catalog is on the table — add a "pending SP inventory" caveat to Section 10 of the brief. (5) Applies to any stack with legacy DB coupling: PHP + stored procs, Java + procs, Python + procs, old Perl CGI + procs. Reference: LEGACY_RE_BRIEF_ES.md Section 9 + Section 11 risk table.

497. **When a repo handover includes URL.txt (or REPO.txt) pointer files, clone INTO the folder next to each pointer — not into a custom scaffold** — On 2026-04-22 the handover dropped `Archiving System/URL.txt` and `Electronic Services/URL.txt`, each pointing to a GitHub repo. The user's intended layout was `{subfolder}/TT{System}/` as a sibling of URL.txt. I cloned into `Archiving System/_legacy_audit/TT*` instead, which forced a mid-session move + sed rewrites across MD + HTML + JSON + `_refresh.sh` (cost ~40 min of rework). **Why:** pointer files are an implicit contract — they say "the repo belongs here." Ignoring that creates two divergent folder trees, confuses future refresh scripts, and forces hot-swap moves once the user notices. **How to apply:** (1) Before any `git clone`, scan the working directory and its sibling directories for `URL.txt`, `REPO.txt`, `repo-url.txt`, `.repo`, or any empty folder whose name matches the repo name in the URL — that's the target. (2) If an empty placeholder folder exists at the target, clone into it (`git clone <url> <empty-dir>` works on existing empty dirs). (3) If a sibling already contains a populated clone from a parallel Claude session, use it as canonical — do not duplicate. (4) Save the `_refresh.sh`-style refresh paths as CONFIG in a JSON file (not hardcoded into the script), so a move costs one JSON edit not three files. (5) If the user's URL.txt says repo A goes at location X and you want to clone elsewhere, ASK — don't substitute.

498. **Commercial NuGet / pip / npm packages in dependency manifests are hidden rebuild cost drivers — scan dependency files early, surface in the scoping document** — On 2026-04-22, `TTArchivingSystem/UIWeb/packages.config` declared `Aspose.Words 19.4` (commercial annual license) + `LPTPrinting` / `LPTPrinting32` VB-authored DLLs (legacy dot-matrix/line printer support — hardware-binding). Both change 5-year TCO materially and are easy to miss in a first-pass "what framework is this?" scan. **Why:** a migration estimate built from LOC + framework + DB engine alone will miss the annual Aspose renewal cost AND the hardware-printer preserve-mode engineering that the rewrite must replicate. Both items belong in the Decision Matrix TCO row and the Risk Register. **How to apply:** (1) For any .NET-legacy assessment, grep every `packages.config` + `*.csproj` `<PackageReference>` for: `Aspose`, `DevExpress`, `Telerik`, `Syncfusion`, `ComponentOne`, `Infragistics`, `EPPlus` (commercial since v5), `GemBox`, `ActiveReports`, `Stimulsoft`, `Iron*` (IronPdf, IronOcr), `Spire.*`. (2) For Python: check `requirements.txt` / `pyproject.toml` for `reportlab` (commercial for advanced features), `PyPDF2`-vs-`pypdf`, any `*-pro` or `*-enterprise` suffix. (3) For Node: `package.json` for `@devexpress/*`, `@telerik/*`, any `-pro` suffix. (4) Also check for legacy printing/scanning DLLs (LPT*, TWAIN*, WIA*) — these imply hardware coupling and preserve-mode work. (5) Flag every hit in a "Hidden Commercial / Hardware Dependencies" section of the rebuild-scoping document with: current license cost, replacement open-source option, replacement engineering effort in weeks.

499. **Runtime dynamic form builders (Blueprint + BlueprintField pattern) do NOT map to Odoo Studio — this is the #1 Odoo gap for DMS / ESM / eGov migrations** — Odoo Studio is **design-time**: an admin drags fields in a UI and the schema persists. The Blueprint/BlueprintField pattern in the 2026-04-22 TTElectronicServices code is **runtime**: field definitions live as data rows (`Blueprint`, `BlueprintField`, `BlueprintFieldValidation`, `BlueprintSubmitValidation`), rendered on-the-fly per request, with validation rules evaluated from DB. Odoo coverage for this domain in the v1 analysis: **35 %**. **Why:** many government and enterprise legacy systems (Qatar Charity, ministries, banks) use this runtime pattern because non-IT staff author forms without deploying code. If the rebuild recommendation is Odoo, Studio cannot cover it without a heavy custom module that reinvents Form.io — which collapses the "Odoo covers X %" argument. **How to apply:** (1) When auditing any DMS / eGov / ESM / service-request legacy system, search entity names for `Blueprint`, `FormDefinition`, `DynamicField`, `FieldSchema`, `FormTemplate` — match on runtime-form pattern. (2) If found, add a "Dynamic Form Builder — Runtime vs Design-time" block to the Odoo Coverage Map. (3) Recommend **Form.io**, **django-forms-builder**, or **formio-builder** on the Django/Next side — they map 1:1 to the runtime pattern. (4) Flag this explicitly in the Decision Matrix under "Customizability — hard domains" — it's usually the tie-breaker. (5) Do not describe Studio as "equivalent" — call it "design-time only" and quote the gap %.

500. **Outlook .msg attachments can show 0 bytes while the actual file exists as a sibling in the parent folder — always check before declaring an attachment missing** — On 2026-04-22, Basha's `.msg` (extracted via Outlook COM) reported `Archive-ES-SolutionPages.xlsx (0 bytes)` as the embedded attachment, but a full 26 KB copy existed at `D:\Archiving & Electronic Services System\Archive-ES-SolutionPages.xlsx` as a sibling of the `.msg`. This is an Outlook rendering / COM-projection artifact when an attachment was saved out-of-band, not file corruption. **Why:** a plan built on the assumption "the attachment is missing, we cannot read it" would have been delayed or scoped wrong. The sibling copy was the authoritative source and parsing it unblocked the entire Excel-deep-study phase. **How to apply:** (1) When `.msg.Attachments[i].Size == 0`, do NOT declare the file missing. (2) Search the `.msg`'s parent folder (and one level up) for files matching the attachment's `FileName`. (3) If found, document the quirk in the plan's Assumptions section: "treating the N-byte folder copy as authoritative; .msg embedded copy shows 0 bytes (Outlook artifact)." (4) Save both the `.msg` body-hash AND the folder-copy-hash into your change-detection metadata (`msg_metadata.json`) so future refresh can detect new emails from the same thread. (5) This also applies to embedded OLE objects and linked files in Word/Excel — always probe the parent folder for real siblings before giving up. (6) Specific COM invocation: `New-Object -ComObject Outlook.Application; $ns.OpenSharedItem($path); foreach ($a in $msg.Attachments) { Write-Output "$($a.FileName) ($($a.Size) bytes)" }` — the Size field is the unreliable one.

501. **Windows empty-but-locked folder: use `git clone <url> <empty-dir>` instead of fighting the lock** — On 2026-04-22 an empty placeholder folder (`Archiving System/TTArchivingSystem/`, 0 files, no `.git`) refused both `rmdir` ("Device or resource busy") and PowerShell `Remove-Item -Recurse -Force` ("Cannot remove the item ... because it is in use"). Root cause was a terminal `cd`'d into the folder OR Windows Explorer selecting it — leaving a handle that `handle.exe` wasn't in PATH to identify. **Why:** the standard fix ("find the process and close it") is time-costly and needs user cooperation. `git clone <url> <dir>` allows cloning into an existing empty directory (Git 2.14+), so you can populate the folder WITHOUT first deleting it. Saved ~15 min of "close your VS Code / Explorer / terminal" ping-pong. **How to apply:** (1) When a Windows folder operation blocks with "Device or resource busy" / "in use" and the folder is empty or mostly-empty, try an IN-PLACE operation instead of delete-then-create. (2) For a locked empty target: `git clone <url> "<locked-empty-path>"` — works. (3) For a locked partially-populated target: move content OUT file-by-file (usually a few files aren't locked even if the folder root handle is), then clone in. (4) For a locked folder that's neither empty nor clone-able: rename the PARENT folder (Rename-Item sometimes bypasses handle locks that Remove-Item cannot), then work with the renamed copy. (5) General principle: on Windows, prefer in-place transforms (write-over, clone-into, rename-parent) over delete-then-create. (6) Cross-reference: OS-level `handle.exe` from Sysinternals identifies the locking process, but is rarely worth installing mid-session.

502. **Parallel Claude sessions in the same working tree — detect by mtime, never assume unknown files are mine** — On 2026-04-22 during a 2-hour session, new files appeared in the project tree that I did not create: `LEGACY_RE_BRIEF_ES.md` + `.html` at `Electronic Services/` (34 KB + 60 KB), a `_inventory/` folder with 5 analysis `.md` files inside `TTElectronicServices/`, and a fresh `CLAUDE.md` at the parent root. These were from a parallel Claude session the user was running concurrently. Twice I almost touched them before checking mtime. **Why:** auto mode + multiple Claude windows open on the same project is a normal user pattern. If I assume every new file is mine and edit or rename it, I destroy the parallel session's work AND confuse the user about which session owns what. **How to apply:** (1) On entering a long session, record a mtime baseline: `find <workdir> -newer /tmp/claude-session-start.marker` (or equivalent). (2) Every 30 min or after each major phase, re-run `ls -la` and compare — any file with mtime AFTER my last known-edit-to-that-file needs classification. (3) Classify unknown-origin files into three buckets: (a) user's manual work → leave alone, (b) parallel Claude session output → read for context but do not edit, (c) tool artifact (linter, IDE) → usually safe to leave. Ask the user when (a) vs (b) is unclear. (4) Cross-reference `_inventory/`-style analysis folders as the user's parallel-session output that COMPLEMENTS mine — they are peers to my deliverable, not inputs I own. (5) Before any `rm -rf` or bulk `Edit`, re-check the folder manifest. (6) On the summary deliverable, note: "parallel artefact detected at X — NOT modified by this session" so the user has a clean handoff trail. (7) If the parallel session has produced a competing deliverable (same topic, different angle), propose a reconcile step before either ships to stakeholders — do not silently assume mine supersedes.

## Single-File HTML Apps & Browser-Direct Azure DevOps Pattern (Apr 27, 2026)

503. **Default to browser-direct PAT pattern (single HTML, no PS server) for any new local Azure DevOps tool — server-backed is legacy** — On 2026-04-27 the PBI Subtask App was first built as 3 files (Run-Subtask-Server.ps1 + Start-App.bat + HTML on localhost:5060) following the Outlook v9-v13 listener pattern. User immediately asked to collapse it to ONE HTML file calling Azure DevOps REST directly from the browser with PAT auth. The collapse worked end-to-end: GET (fetch work item) AND PATCH (create child task with JSON-Patch body) both pass through CORS from `file://` with `Authorization: Basic btoa(':' + PAT)`. **Why:** server-backed adds 3 moving parts (PS window must stay open, port management, BAT launcher), zero benefit for read+write Azure DevOps operations once you accept PAT-in-localStorage auth. Server pattern is appropriate ONLY when the tool needs file-system access beyond File API, an unauthenticated origin proxy, or executing CLI tools (e.g., `az` for `az rest`). **How to apply:** (1) For any new local PM/DevOps tool against `dev.azure.com/TaqaTechno`, default to single-HTML browser-direct. (2) Reuse the localStorage key `devops-pat` workspace-wide — same key the OKR v7, Weekly Meeting v7, and PM Handling v2/v3 dashboards use, so user pastes the PAT once and it works in every dashboard. (3) Auth helpers to copy verbatim (from `D:\Project Management\OKR & KPI\OKR_KPI_Dashboard_v7.html` lines 2586-2587): `function getPAT() { return localStorage.getItem('devops-pat') || ''; }` and `function getAuthHeader() { return 'Basic ' + btoa(':' + getPAT()); }`. (4) PAT modal pattern at lines 1640-1650 of OKR v7 — paste-to-localStorage, show last-4-chars confirmation. (5) Read pattern: `GET https://dev.azure.com/{org}/{project}/_apis/wit/workitems/{id}?$expand=all&api-version=7.1`. (6) Write pattern: `POST https://dev.azure.com/{org}/{project}/_apis/wit/workitems/$Task?api-version=7.1` with `Content-Type: application/json-patch+json` and a 5-op JSON-Patch body for Title / AssignedTo / IterationPath / AreaPath / Hierarchy-Reverse parent link. (7) Required PAT scope for write: **Work Items: Read & write**. (8) CORS fallback if `file://` is blocked by a hardened browser policy: `python -m http.server 8000` from the folder, open via `http://localhost:8000/...html` — same HTML, different origin, no code change. (9) Server-backed pattern (Outlook v9-v13, OKR Data Gate `_run_data_gate.sh`) stays valid for tools that genuinely need shell execution; do not migrate those to browser-direct without need.

504. **"Our PBI subtask approach" lives in TWO files, not one — sprint-plan.md + create-subtasks.ps1 must be read together** — On 2026-04-27, building the PBI Subtask App, RAG returned `~/.claude/plugins-main/devops-plugin/devops/sprint-plan.md` as the canonical "approach" doc. It defines the 6 templates (`[Dev]`, `[Front]`, `[Front Bug Fixing]`, `[QC Test Execution]`, `[QC Bug Fixing]`, `[IMP]`) and a generic role-default mapping (e.g., `[Dev]` → Shehab Gamal). But `D:\DevOps\create-subtasks.ps1` overrides this for BMS (uses Hala Ibrahim, not Shehab) and Wallet's QC routes to Afthab P A (not Mostafa Ahmed). **Why:** if a future tool extracts "the approach" from MD alone, it would assign BMS Dev tasks to the wrong developer (Shehab, who doesn't work BMS) and Wallet QC to Mostafa (who doesn't dedicate to Wallet). MD has the framework; PS1 has the project-specific routing rules that MD never absorbed. **How to apply:** (1) When user references "our PBI subtask approach" or "sprint plan approach", read BOTH `~/.claude/plugins-main/devops-plugin/devops/sprint-plan.md` AND any PS1 scripts under `D:\DevOps\` matching `create-subtasks*.ps1` / `*subtask*.ps1` / `Preview-Subtask*.ps1`. (2) Treat the PS1 as authoritative for projects it explicitly covers; treat MD defaults as authoritative for projects not in any PS1. (3) Currently encoded overrides (as of Apr 2026): BMS Dev = Hala (not Shehab); Wallet QC Test + QC Bug = Afthab P A (not Mostafa); all other projects = MD defaults. (4) Capture this in any new PBI-touching tool's project catalog as JS/PS1 constants, NOT inline strings — so adding/changing a project rule is one place. (5) When MD and PS1 disagree, the PS1 wins because it's the actually-running script; flag the divergence to the user once, then update sprint-plan.md to absorb the override (long-term path: MD = single source of truth, PS1 = thin wrapper).

505. **Inline SVG data-URI favicon preserves the "single-file HTML" guarantee** — On 2026-04-27 user asked for a browser-tab favicon on the PBI Subtask App without breaking the single-file deliverable. Solution: `<link rel="icon" type="image/svg+xml" href="data:image/svg+xml;utf8,<svg ...>...</svg>">` with the SVG URL-encoded inline (escape `<` → `%3C`, `>` → `%3E`, `#` → `%23`; use single-quotes inside the SVG for attributes to avoid `"` escaping). **Why:** any sibling `.ico` / `.png` favicon would break the "double-click one HTML and go" promise, force the user to track an extra file, and silently fail when the HTML is moved without its sibling. Inline SVG scales infinitely (no rasterization), works in every modern browser, and survives email/Slack attachment-share intact. **How to apply:** (1) For any single-file HTML deliverable that needs a tab icon, use inline SVG data URI in the `<head>`. (2) Design at viewBox `0 0 64 64` for sharp 16-32-64 px rendering. (3) URL-encode `<`, `>`, `#`. Single-quote SVG attributes to dodge `"` escaping. Spaces, slashes, equals signs need no encoding inside `;utf8,` data URIs. (4) Color palette tip: match the brand color used elsewhere in the HTML (e.g., the primary button) for visual cohesion. (5) Hard-cap SVG size at ~600 chars URL-encoded — anything bigger and the page-source diff noise outweighs the favicon value; switch to base64 PNG instead. (6) PNG fallback for browsers older than IE-edge-2017 is a separate `<link rel="icon" type="image/png">` line — almost never needed in 2026. (7) Cross-reference: same pattern works for app-icon emoji approximation (`<text>` SVG with a single Unicode emoji at large font-size) when a vector design is overkill.



## MD-to-HTML Parser Pitfalls (Apr 27, 2026)

506. **Greedy `\s*$` at end of a heading regex consumes line breaks past the heading when the input has mixed CRLF/LF endings — use `[ \t]*$` instead** — On 2026-04-27 the Phase 3 HTML-builder script `_apply_snapshot_refresh.js` parsed `_portfolio_narratives.md` to extract `## ProjectName` and `### Where/Next/Risk` sections. The regex `^##\s+(.+?)\s*$/gm` was used for project-heading detection. With mixed line endings (Promote-Digests writes CRLF; Inject-ArTranslations.ps1 wrote LF only via PowerShell `` `n ``), `\s*$` greedy-matched `\r\n\r` (CR + LF + next-line CR) instead of just `\r`. This added 2 chars to `pm[0].length`, which shifted `starts[i+1].idx` forward by 2. Boundary subtraction (`starts[i+1].idx - "## NAME\n".length`) was off by 2, leaking `##` from the next project's heading into the EN narrative content. **Why:** `\s` matches all whitespace INCLUDING `\r` and `\n`. With `m` flag, `$` anchors to before any `\n`, so a greedy `\s*` will happily consume across multiple lines until it finds an endpoint where `$` is satisfied — typically the next `\n`. The bug is silent on pure-LF or pure-CRLF input but blows up on mixed input. **How to apply:** (1) For any line-anchored heading regex, use `[ \t]*$` (horizontal-only whitespace) instead of `\s*$`. (2) When extracting "everything after this heading until before the next heading," prefer storing `pm.index` of the next heading and using THAT directly as the end boundary — never subtract a string-template length, that's brittle to line-ending differences. (3) Detect mixed line endings on input early: `(crlfCount, lfOnlyCount)` — if both > 0, normalize first via `.replace(/\r\n/g, '\n')` before parsing. (4) PowerShell scripts that emit MD must explicitly use `"\r`n"` in string literals if the surrounding file is CRLF, OR explicitly normalize via `Set-Content -NoNewline` + manual line endings. (5) In Node-side parser tests, run on three fixtures: pure-LF, pure-CRLF, mixed (LF inside CRLF). (6) Verbose-debug helper that catches this fast: `console.log('pm0Last:', JSON.stringify(pm[0].slice(-3)))` — a value like `"\r\n\r"` immediately reveals the over-consume. Reference: `_apply_snapshot_refresh.js` parseNarrativesMd + parseProjectSections.

507. **Detect parenthesized header variants (e.g., `(AR)`, `(EN)`, `(draft)`, `(new)`) BEFORE stripping parens — strip-then-match destroys disambiguation** — On 2026-04-27 `normaliseHeader('Where (AR)')` was implemented as: lowercase → `s.replace(/\([^)]*\)/g, '')` → switch on cleaned string. This stripped `(AR)` before the AR check ran, so `Where (AR)` reduced to `where` and was treated as a duplicate of EN `Where`. Result: AR fields stayed empty across all 24 cards because the duplicate-merge logic kept the first occurrence (the EN one). **Why:** generic strip-parens handles "decorative" annotations like `(new)`, `(previous)`, `(draft)` that should NOT change the field's identity. But `(AR)` IS the identity discriminator — stripping it collapses two distinct fields into one. **How to apply:** (1) In any header normalizer, run identity-bearing-suffix tests FIRST: `if (/^where\s*\(ar\)$/.test(s)) return 'whereAr'`, similarly for `(en)`, language tags, kind tags. (2) Only AFTER those tests, strip generic decorative parens. (3) Same rule applies to filename normalizers (`report-v2.docx` vs `report (final).docx` — version vs annotation), URL slug builders, and any parser that uses suffix-stripping. (4) Test fixture: feed the parser pairs of inputs differing only in the identity suffix and assert they produce DIFFERENT keys. (5) The bug surfaces silently as "data missing" — the cleaner symptom — so include "every field expected to be populated IS populated" assertions in any post-parse validator. Reference: `_apply_snapshot_refresh.js` normaliseHeader, fixed Apr 27.

508. **Heading-content boundary calc: prefer `nextHeading.pmIndex` directly over `nextHeading.startAfter - "### NAME\n".length` subtraction** — On 2026-04-27 the parser computed each section's content end as `endIdx = hits[i+1].start - ('### ' + hits[i+1].title + '\n').length` where `hits[i+1].start = pm.index + pm[0].length`. The subtraction was supposed to walk back from "after the next heading" to "right before the next heading". But the walk-back used a hand-built template string (`### NAME\n`) whose length didn't match what `pm[0]` actually consumed — the regex's greedy `\s*$` consumed `\r` too, making `pm[0].length` larger than the template length. Net effect: section content boundary off by N chars where N = `pm[0].length - template.length` (typically 1-3). **Why:** any time you compute "position before X" by subtracting a hand-coded length from "position after X", you're assuming what the regex consumed equals what you're subtracting. That assumption breaks the moment the regex matches `\r` or trailing whitespace beyond the heading text. **How to apply:** (1) Always store `pm.index` (the position WHERE the next heading STARTS) alongside `pm.index + pm[0].length` in the hits array. (2) Use `nextHit.pmIndex` directly as the previous section's `endIdx` — no subtraction. (3) This idiom is robust across all encodings, line endings, and trailing-whitespace policies. (4) Same bug class affects: chunk-extraction in chunkers, comment-strippers, MD-section walkers, code-fence parsers. Anywhere you write `start_of_next - "delimiter".length` to find "end of previous", refactor to "use start_of_next directly." (5) Memoize: "boundary by next-start-position, never by next-content-length-subtraction." Reference: `_apply_snapshot_refresh.js` fixed Apr 27 — both outer (project) and inner (subsection) boundary calcs migrated.

509. **When the same key (like a project name) appears in BOTH a runtime helper map AND the main data object, scope all `findBlock(html, key)` lookups to the main object's bounds — never search the whole HTML** — On 2026-04-27 the Phase 3 HTML-builder injected an `ALAQRABOON_CARD_MAP` helper near the top of the `<script>` block: `window.ALAQRABOON_CARD_MAP = { 'KhairGate - Alaqraboon Center': { devops: '...', areaPath: '...' }, ... }`. Later, the same key `'KhairGate - Alaqraboon Center': {` appears inside `var PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES = {` further down. When Phase 3 was re-run on the previous version of the HTML to apply a new snapshot, `findProjectBlock(html, 'KhairGate - Alaqraboon Center')` did `html.indexOf('\'KhairGate - Alaqraboon Center\': {')` and found the HELPER occurrence FIRST (lower file offset). It then balance-matched the closing brace and replaced the helper's `{ devops, areaPath }` config with the new EN+AR narrative block, corrupting the helper AND leaving the actual `PORTFOLIO_NARRATIVES` entry untouched. Symptom: dashboard's Portfolio Status renders Center/Wallet cards with empty `whereAr/nextAr/riskAr`. **Why:** any HTML that mixes "config maps" and "data maps" using JS object literals with the same key name will hit this collision the moment a generic key-search runs. The first run is fine because no helpers exist yet; the SECOND run (after helpers were injected) is when the bug manifests — making this a "self-corrupting" subtle bug that passes naive testing. **How to apply:** (1) Before any `findBlock(html, key)` lookup, locate the bounds of the target object first: `findBoundsOf('var DATA_MAP = {')` returns `{startIdx, endIdx}`. (2) Pass those bounds to `findBlock`, which uses `html.indexOf(marker, bounds.startIdx)` and rejects matches `> bounds.endIdx`. (3) Generalize the rule: ANY HTML or JS file that gets injected with auxiliary data structures (helper maps, manifest arrays, config objects) needs scoped lookups for ALL key-based mutations. (4) Detection: if your build script injects a helper that uses object keys matching your data keys, wrap a regression test that runs the script TWICE on the same input and asserts the helper's structure is unchanged after the second run. (5) Alternative architecture: prefix helper keys to make them disjoint from data keys (e.g., `'card:KhairGate - Alaqraboon Center'` in the helper, plain key in the data). Less safe than scoped lookups (relies on convention). (6) For the JS object case specifically, balance-match the OUTER object's `{...}` once on first encounter, cache the bounds, then ALL subsequent inner-key lookups go through the bounds-restricted helper. Reference: `_apply_snapshot_refresh.js` `findPortfolioNarrativesBounds` + `findProjectBlock`, fixed Apr 27.

## Git Merge Strategy & Pre-Merge Inspection (Apr 27, 2026)
