638. **Same-tab governance affordance pattern: indicator tabs (SpiCpi, DataGate, etc.) get an "⚡ Auto-draft red rows" header button + per-row "covered/uncovered" pill that mirror the Action Log state — reuse `buildXDrafts` + `buildBlockingTuples` rather than duplicating the pipeline** — On 2026-05-04 closing parity gap #7 (governance hooks: in v14 the Action Log auto-draft + SLA + L1/L2/L3 escalation logic lived in OTHER tabs, not the SPI/CPI tab; same gap existed in PMO_OS). Closed it with two visible affordances: (a) header button "⚡ Auto-draft red rows" calls `buildSpiDrafts(rows, blockingTuples) + buildCpiDrafts(rows, blockingTuples)` then `await createActionMutation.mutateAsync(d)` for each; (b) per-row "Governance" column shows one of three pill states: 🟡 covered (`SPI L1 · 3d open`), ⚠ uncovered (red row, no entry yet), or em-dash (green row, no action needed). The pills index `openActions` by `(indicator|project)` key for O(1) lookup. **Why:** Forcing the user to context-switch to the Action Log tab to draft an entry from a red metric breaks flow. The governance loop should be initiated where the metric is read. AND duplicating the auto-draft logic creates two pipelines that drift over time. The clean pattern is: indicator tab REUSES the existing `buildSpiDrafts/buildCpiDrafts/buildBlockingTuples` helpers (same ones the Action Log auto-draft button uses), with `buildBlockingTuples(openActions)` ensuring no double-drafting. The per-row pill makes the binding visible — user sees "this red SPI is already at L1, 3 days old" and doesn't redraft. **How to apply:** (1) Any indicator tab that surfaces zone-classified rows MUST have a same-tab "auto-draft red rows" button that reuses the canonical draft builders. (2) Build a `Record<string, ActionLogState>` keyed by `${indicator}|${project}` (or equivalent dedup key) and render a per-row pill that shows the open entry's level + days. (3) Pill color: amber for "covered" (an open entry exists), red for "uncovered" (red row needs draft), muted for "no action" (green row). (4) The header button MUST report skipped count: `created N, skipped M (already covered)` — confirms the dedup actually worked. (5) NEVER write a parallel auto-draft pipeline — the 5-indicator pipeline lives in `auto-draft.ts` + Action Log; the indicator tabs are CALLERS, not implementers. (6) Reference: May 4 — `D:\PMO_OS\src\components\SpiCpi.tsx:114-189` `autoDraftRedRows()` + `openActionByKey` index + `Governance` column with `govPillCovered`/`govPillUncovered`/`govPillNone` i18n keys.

639. **Empty-state for filtered-bucket sections must be affirmatively visible (✓ green panel with "what populates this") not muted italic — users mistake muted-italic empty-state for "section is missing" and report bugs** — On 2026-05-04 user feedback after first SpiCpi tab demo: "Projects with Tasks but Data Gap is not appear". The section header WAS rendering with count=0, followed by italic muted text "No projects in this state." User read the muted italic as "the section is broken / not loading". Fix: changed empty-state styling from `bg-bg/40 border-border/50 italic text-fg-muted` (muted, easy-to-miss) to `bg-zone-green/5 border-zone-green/30 text-zone-green` with a leading ✓ checkmark and an explanation of what triggers population (e.g., "every active project has more than 5 closed actual hours"). Result: empty section now reads as "this section IS here AND the data is healthy" rather than "looks broken". **Why:** Visual hierarchy communicates loading/empty/error states. Muted italic reads as "placeholder, nothing to see" — same affordance as a disabled button. Affirmative green reads as "this is the working state, and it's good." Users who report "X not appearing" are usually saying "X is rendering but the styling told me it's not active." **How to apply:** (1) NEVER style an empty-bucket section with muted italic + low-opacity border. (2) Use ✓ green panel ("affirmatively healthy") for filter buckets that are intentionally empty — e.g., "no projects with gaps", "no failing tests", "no SLA breaches". (3) Always include a one-line explanation of what would populate the section, so the user understands the criteria. (4) Reserve the italic-muted style for genuinely irrelevant or "future content" zones, NOT for "filter matched zero rows". (5) Apply the same rule across all bucket-style sections (DataGate's clean projects group, Risk Register's no-active-risks state, etc.). (6) Reference: May 4 — `SpiCpi.tsx` Data Gap empty state: `<div className="bg-zone-green/5 border-zone-green/30 text-zone-green">✓ {t('groupGapEmpty')}</div>`.

640. **v14's strict `closedActual === 0` filter for "Data Gap" hides thin-sample projects whose CPI is already noise — broaden to `closedActual ≤ 5h` so small/new projects surface and don't pollute the Metrics group with unreliable indicators** — On 2026-05-04 user feedback flagged Data Gap section as "not populating" even though the section was rendering. Investigation: every active project in the dataset had ≥1h of closed actuals, so v14's strict filter (`closedActual === 0` = "CPI uncomputable") matched ZERO rows. But projects like AI Call Center (4h closed), IndoQat (3h closed), Taqat (1h closed), Taqat Facilities (1h closed) had CPI numbers based on so few hours that the indicator was meaningless — yet they were grouped with Wallet (1593h closed) and Pearl Pixels (1333h closed) in the Metrics table as if their CPIs were equally trustworthy. Lesson #225 already said "below 5h closed, CPI is noise." Fix: broadened filter to `r.planned > 0 && r.closedActual <= 5` (using ≤5 so the 5h threshold itself counts as thin-sample). 4 projects moved from Metrics → Data Gap; section now visibly populated. **Why:** v14's strict filter optimizes for "uncomputable" (literal /0) but ignores the broader category of "computed but statistically meaningless." A CPI of 1.0 derived from 1 closed task is not a healthy CPI; it's the only sample we have. Users reading the Metrics table can't distinguish "CPI 1.0 from 200 closed tasks" (real signal) from "CPI 1.0 from 1 closed task" (random outcome). Surfacing thin-samples in Data Gap forces the conversation. **How to apply:** (1) When porting filters from a legacy artifact, check whether the strict version captures the meaningful population. Sometimes the spec was right but the threshold needs rebasing (e.g., from "0" to "≤ N where N is the noise floor"). (2) Document the deviation from the source artifact in a code comment AND in the i18n strings so users see "PMO_OS broader than v14" not "PMO_OS broken vs v14". (3) When broadening, deduplicate downstream — projects in Data Gap MUST NOT also appear in Metrics. Build a `Set<string>` of data-gap project names and filter Metrics with `!dataGapKeys.has(r.project)`. (4) Pick the threshold from existing project lessons (here #225's "5h"), not arbitrary. (5) Reference: May 4 — `SpiCpi.tsx` `DATA_GAP_THIN_SAMPLE_THRESHOLD = 5` constant + dedup via `dataGapKeys` Set; i18n hint updated to "planned hours but ≤5h closed actuals — CPI not computable or sample too thin to trust."

641. **Page placement of "How to read this tab" expandable info panel: BOTTOM-anchored beats TOP-anchored — at the bottom it answers "wait, what does this column mean?" the moment the user encounters something unfamiliar; at the top it interrupts the read-the-data flow with a wall of explanation that gets dismissed and never re-opened** — On 2026-05-04 after first SpiCpi tab demo, user explicitly asked: "move the How to read this tab in below in the page". Initial placement was TOP (right under the header), reasoning was "explain before showing". User's reasoning for BOTTOM: by the time the user has scrolled through the metrics, sparkline, governance pills, group sections, they've already encountered every column and concept — the panel becomes a "click here if anything was unclear" reference, not a tutorial gate. TOP placement creates noise on the read-the-data path; BOTTOM placement makes the panel discoverable when needed without forcing a context interruption. **Why:** Information panels positioned BEFORE the data force every user (including expert users who already know the conventions) to skim/dismiss the panel before reaching the data. Information panels positioned AFTER the data are encountered only by users who scrolled to the bottom looking for help — i.e., users who need it. The opt-in flow is better than the opt-out flow. (`<details>` collapsed by default minimizes the visual weight either way, but bottom placement is still cleaner.) **How to apply:** (1) Default placement for "How to read this tab" / "Glossary" / "About these metrics" panels: BOTTOM of the section, after all data renders. (2) Exception: TOP placement is correct ONLY when the data itself cannot be read without the explanation (e.g., abbreviations that have no obvious meaning). For tabs with clear column headers + zone colors, BOTTOM is right. (3) Use `<details>` (collapsed by default) regardless of placement — the panel should never be open at first paint. (4) The summary line should be `ⓘ How to read this tab` with an "click to expand"/"click to collapse" hint, so users know it's interactive. (5) Reference: May 4 — `SpiCpi.tsx` panel was relocated from line ~335 (under header) to line ~722 (just before `</section>`); user confirmed "this is the right place" without further iteration.

## v14 → PMO_OS Outlook Categories Governance Port (May 5, 2026)

642. **Porting localStorage-keyed governance to SQLite: keep the same idempotency key (here, `emailKey`) but elevate it from a JSON-property dedup check to a database-level filter — the rule looks identical at the call site, but execution moves from "scan-the-array" to "WHERE clause" and now scales beyond the 5MB localStorage cap** — On 2026-05-05 porting v14 PMO Dashboard's `autoDraftEmailEntries` + `autoCloseResolvedEmailDrafts` (both keyed by `emailKey`) into PMO_OS Electron. v14 used `pmo-action-log-v1` localStorage with a JS array iterated to find duplicates; PMO_OS uses Drizzle ORM + SQLite via tRPC. The naive port replaced the array with a SQL `select * from action_log` and then iterated in JS, which technically works but throws away the durability win of moving to SQLite. The right port: change `buildBlockingTuples` to accept the same dedup field (`emailKey`) on its lite-row type, and add a tRPC `closeResolvedEmail` procedure that does the closure check server-side via `WHERE scope='email' AND indicator=$ind`. The dedup logic at the call site looks unchanged (`tuples.has(key)`), but the data lives in a transactional store and the closure runs as a single SQL pass. **Why:** Porting a feature without re-thinking its storage layer leaves you with v13-shape code on top of v2 infrastructure — same bugs, same scaling cliff, plus an extra layer to debug. The localStorage version of `autoCloseResolvedEmailDrafts` had to load the full array, mutate in place, JSON.stringify, write back. The SQLite version closes via UPDATE `WHERE id=...` per resolved row — atomic, durable, bounded. Same key, different execution model. Same correctness guarantees, plus durability. **How to apply:** (1) When porting governance/dedup logic across storage backends, identify the **idempotency key** first and lift it INTO the schema (here: `emailKey TEXT` on `action_log`). (2) Keep the BUILDER function pure: it returns drafts based on a `Set<string>` of blocking tuples. The CALLER is responsible for sourcing those tuples from the new backend. (3) Add a closure procedure server-side instead of replicating the v13 client-side mutation loop — `closeResolvedEmail({indicator, currentEmailKeys})` reads open rows once, computes the diff, issues UPDATEs. (4) Wire it into the existing sync flow: `Promise.all([...drafts.map(create), close('email-reply', keys), close('email-mention', keys)])`. (5) Update the i18n success string to surface `closed: N` so the user sees both halves of the loop fire (`Synced X reply + Y mention · auto-closed Z resolved`). (6) Reference: May 5 — PMO_OS `auto-draft.ts` + `router.ts` `closeResolvedEmail` procedure + `ActionLog.tsx` `syncEmailIndicators`; the port took ~3h end-to-end including 22 new Vitest tests with zero baseline regressions (203 → 248 tests).

643. **`emailKey` strategy is asymmetric per indicator and that asymmetry IS the rule — sender-based dedup for reply tracking (one row per pending sender), thread-based dedup for missed mentions (one row per thread). Don't unify them or you'll either spam or under-count** — On 2026-05-05 porting v14:13452 logic into PMO_OS `buildEmailReplyDrafts` and `buildEmailMentionDrafts`. v14 chooses the key with this branch: `(kind === 'mentions') ? normalizeThreadKey(subject) : (fromEmail || from).toLowerCase().trim()`. The asymmetry isn't an oversight — it reflects what the user is being asked to do. For email-reply: the user owes a sender N replies; one outstanding row per sender (collapsing multiple emails from the same person into one entry) keeps the Action Log uncluttered. For email-mention: the user owes the thread a response; one row per thread (so Re:/Fw: collapse together) is right. If you flip them — sender-key on mentions or thread-key on replies — you either get one row for every email-thread mention from a single person (spam) or you collapse multiple replies-needed senders into one row when they happen to share a Re: prefix (under-count). **Why:** The dedup key reflects the unit-of-action. Reply-owing is per-sender (you reply to a person about anything they sent); mention-owing is per-thread (you respond to one specific conversation). Different units = different keys. (`normalizeThreadKey` strips Re:/Fw:/Fwd:/Res: + Arabic رد:/إعادة توجيه: prefixes so threads collapse correctly even across mixed-language replies.) **How to apply:** (1) When implementing dedup for any "obligation tracker", identify the unit-of-action first. (2) Mirror v14's helper structure: `mapEmailCategoryToProject(category) -> string|null` (first non-empty token from `;`/`,`-split list) + `normalizeThreadKey(subject) -> string` (lowercase, strip prefixes). Both pure functions, both easy to unit-test. (3) When category is missing, surface as alarm (`'⚠️ UNCATEGORIZED — assign category in Outlook'` + `emailUncategorized=true` flag) instead of inventing a fallback project name — this enforces user-side discipline and avoids polluting the project register with auto-coined names. (4) Test both helpers exhaustively: empty, null, whitespace, semicolon-delimited, comma-delimited, stacked English prefixes, Arabic prefixes, mixed EN+AR. (5) Reference: May 5 — PMO_OS `auto-draft.ts:467+517` builders + `auto-draft.test.ts:262+` tests; lesson #596 (May 1) is the parent — "trust user-curated source-of-truth labels over canonical lookup tables".

644. **SLA threshold drift between v13 prose, v14 implementation, and PMO_OS port: pick ONE threshold, anchor it to the source-of-truth artifact, and document the cascade — if v14 says `hoursOld > 96` and your port still says `>= 120`, you're 24 hours late on every red signal** — On 2026-05-05 implementing the email-reply zone classifier. PMO_OS had `function emailZone(h) { return h >= 120 ? 'red' : 'amber'; }` (5 workdays past SLA). v14 has `pastSla = hoursOld > 96` (3 workdays past SLA). The 24h drift would have meant the Action Log ringing the alarm a full day later than the source dashboard. Fixed `emailZone` to `h > 96` and updated tests at 96h/97h boundary. **Why:** When two artifacts implement the "same" governance rule with different thresholds, the artifact in daily use IS the source of truth — even if the documentation said 120h. v14 has been live for weeks; the user's mental model is calibrated to v14's red-fires-at-96h timing. PMO_OS exists to inherit that exact calibration, not to re-derive it from first principles. **How to apply:** (1) When porting numeric thresholds, grep the SOURCE artifact (`hoursOld\s*[><=]+\s*\d+` etc.) for the actual constant in use; don't trust prose like "5 workdays". (2) Add the source-line reference in a code comment: `// L049 (v14 parity, ...) — v14:13459 uses hoursOld > 96`. (3) Test the boundary: 96 = amber, 97 = red — boundary tests catch off-by-one drift instantly. (4) When the user adjusts a threshold in the source artifact, search for `[constant]` in the port and update both — never let the port lag. (5) Reference: May 5 — PMO_OS `auto-draft.ts:489` zone threshold + boundary tests at hoursOld 96/97 and escalation L2 above 168h.

## Outlook Follow-up Flag end-to-end + sidecar hot-reload (May 5, 2026 — afternoon session)

645. **User-marked priority signals (Outlook Flag-for-Followup, P1 ticket badge, "starred" indicators) MUST override SLA-driven zone math — a flag is the user's explicit "act now" gesture and should NOT be diluted by the time-based curve, even when hoursOld is well below the SLA threshold** — On 2026-05-05 wiring `IsFlagged` from `Extract-Inbox.ps1` → PowerShell server → `buildEmailReplyDrafts` / `buildEmailMentionDrafts` (PMO_OS) AND v14 `autoDraftEmailEntries`. First impulse: just append a 🚩 badge to the action text but keep zone driven by `emailZone(hoursOld)`. Wrong. The flag means "I want to see this NOW, regardless of how old it is" — a user can flag a 2-hour-old email and expect it red. So the rule became: `flagged ? 'red' : emailZone(hoursOld)` for the reply builder, AND `flagged ? 'red' : 'amber'` for the mention builder (which has no SLA curve at all and was always amber). Plus `escalationLevel = flagged ? 2 : (hoursOld > 168 ? 2 : hoursOld > 96 ? 1 : 0)`. **Why:** SLA logic is a fallback for unmonitored streams — the system trying to surface what the user hasn't manually triaged. Once the user HAS triaged (flagged it), the SLA's job is done — the flag IS the alarm. Diluting the flag through the SLA curve means flagged emails get the same color as unflagged-but-old emails, which is the worst of both worlds. Hierarchy: explicit user input > automated curve, every time. **How to apply:** (1) Any zone classifier that takes both an automatic metric (hoursOld, ratio, count) and a user-marked flag should apply the flag FIRST and short-circuit: `if (flagged) return 'red';` before the curve runs. (2) Same rule for escalation level — flagged starts at L2 (the highest), not L0. (3) Surface the flag visually with TWO redundant signals: row background tint + a 🚩 badge with tooltip showing the user's flag-request text (Outlook's `FlagRequest` is a free-text field — don't drop it). (4) When grouping/deduping flagged items (e.g., one row per sender, collapsing multiple emails), OR-merge the flag: `mergedFlag = a.isFlagged || b.isFlagged` so a flag on ANY email survives the collapse. Otherwise the dedup throws away the user's explicit signal. (5) Reference: May 5 — `Extract-Inbox.ps1` (FlagStatus=2 OR IsMarkedAsTask → IsFlagged column), `Run-Update-Server.ps1` (OR-merge in sender-dedup pass), `auto-draft.ts` builders (forced red+L2), `OutlookCategories.tsx` (🚩 column + summary card), v14 `autoDraftEmailEntries` (mirror logic).

646. **Test "skip empty input" cases by normalizing the RAW field, not the display fallback — `normalizeThreadKey('(no subject)')` returns a non-empty string so an empty-subject mention sneaks through and creates a row with the placeholder as its dedup key** — On 2026-05-05 first test run after L049 surfaced this exact bug: `buildEmailMentionDrafts` had `const subject = it.subject?.trim() || '(no subject)'; const emailKey = normalizeThreadKey(subject);`. Test `skips items with empty subject (no thread key derivable)` expected length 0; got 1. Root cause: the display-fallback `(no subject)` was applied BEFORE the normalize-and-skip check, so `normalizeThreadKey('(no subject)')` returned `(no subject)` (non-empty), passed the `if (!emailKey) continue;` gate, and emitted a draft with `emailKey: '(no subject)'` — a single shared dedup bucket that would silently collapse every empty-subject mention into one row across all senders. v14 line 13452 normalizes the RAW subject and skips on empty. Fix: split the variables — `emailKey = normalizeThreadKey(it.subject || '')` for the gate check, and `subject = it.subject?.trim() || '(no subject)'` ONLY for display. **Why:** Display fallbacks and dedup keys serve opposite purposes. Display fallback wants "always show something readable" (so the row has a subject column). Dedup key wants "skip when there's no real signal" (so empty-subject items don't all collapse together). When you let the display fallback feed the dedup key, you're treating "no input" as a valid input — silently dangerous. **How to apply:** (1) Always derive dedup keys from the RAW field, before any user-facing fallback. (2) When you have a dedup-vs-display split, name them differently in code (`emailKey` vs `subject`) so they can't be accidentally swapped. (3) Add a test for the empty/null/whitespace input — it's the cheapest test to write and catches this class of bug at compile time. (4) Reference: May 5 — `auto-draft.ts:540` mention builder split into `emailKey = normalizeThreadKey(it.subject || '')` then `if (!emailKey) continue;` then `subject = it.subject?.trim() || '(no subject)'`.

647. **In-app "Restart Sidecar" button beats teaching the user to close-and-reopen the whole Electron app — sidecar lifecycle is already managed by the Electron parent, expose it via a tRPC mutation so the renderer can hot-reload the Python child without bouncing the host** — On 2026-05-05 after editing `sidecar/outlook.py` to emit `is_flagged`, the user asked "how do I restart the sidecar?" The first answer was a multi-step close+reopen procedure (Ctrl+C the dev:all terminal, re-run, etc.). User pushback: "do these steps now". The right move was: stop documenting the workaround, build the button. Added `sidecar.status` (returns {pid, healthy}) + `sidecar.restart` mutation (calls `killSidecar()` → SIGTERM → 2s → SIGKILL → `launchSidecar()` → polls /health with exponential backoff → returns {ok, status}) on the tRPC root, then a 🔄 Restart link on the Settings → Python Sidecar card. Total: ~25 lines of router code + ~15 lines of UI. After this, every `sidecar/*.py` edit is a 1-click hot-reload — no terminal bounce, no full Electron restart, no losing the renderer's tRPC query cache. **Why:** Documentation is a workaround when the host's lifecycle owns a child process the user wants to manage. The tools to control the child (kill + spawn) already exist in `electron/sidecar/launcher.ts` exports — the only thing missing is renderer access. tRPC bridges that gap with no extra IPC plumbing. Documentation says "I gave up; here's a procedure"; the button says "I solved it." When the user asks "how do I X" three times in a session and X is something the system already does internally, the answer is to expose it, not document it. **How to apply:** (1) For any iterative workflow (edit → test → edit → test) where the user has to manually kill+respawn a managed process, build a one-click control instead of teaching the procedure. (2) The tRPC pattern: `<feature>.status` (query) + `<feature>.restart` / `<feature>.runNow` (mutation) — same shape as `backupRouter`, `briefingRouter`, `autoFetchRouter`. (3) Place the button near where the symptom appears: Restart Sidecar lives next to the sidecar health probe on Settings. (4) UX details: show "Restarting…" while pending, then `✓ pid=<pid>` (green) on success or `✗ /health never went green` (red) on failure. Re-probe after success so the card auto-flips to 🟢 Healthy. (5) Reference: May 5 — `src/main/trpc/router.ts` `sidecarRouter` + `src/components/Settings.tsx` Python Sidecar card.

## PMO_OS Velocity / Outlook / Insights v14 Parity Patterns (May 5, 2026 — afternoon-evening session)

648. **When porting a curated lookup table from a working artifact, hardcode the SOURCE table verbatim in the port and use the local DB as a SECONDARY fallback — making the port DB-driven from day one creates a drift class where stale seeds silently produce wrong-team-bucketing on every render** — On 2026-05-05 porting v14 Team Velocity to PMO_OS. First implementation read `team_roster` SQLite table to map assignee→teamCode. Symptom: 7 of 17 contributors landed in ⚠ Unassigned because (a) the seed had only 19 of 30 v14 members, AND (b) `seedTeamRosterIfEmpty()` short-circuited because the DB was non-empty from a prior launch with the OLD seed, so the expanded 30-canonical+19-alias seed never wrote. Fix: created `src/lib/v14-team-mapping.ts` with `V14_ASSIGNEE_TO_TEAM = new Map(...)` mirroring v14:6231 verbatim (47 entries, normalized lowercase keys), and rewrote `teamCodeFor(name)` to a 3-tier lookup: hardcoded v14 map → DB roster → `'un'`. Same pattern applied to `V14_FORMER_MEMBERS` for the `(former)` decoration. **Why:** v14 has been hand-curated for months. The DB seed is downstream of v14, not upstream — when the user moves Hacene from Qatar→Backend in v14, that's the source of truth, and any port that doesn't mirror v14 is wrong by definition. DB-driven approaches sound flexible but in practice introduce a synchronization burden the user shouldn't carry. Hardcoded primary + DB fallback gives you "deterministic by default, extensible when needed" — new hires can land in the DB before v14 catches up, but the 30 known cases are bulletproof. **How to apply:** (1) When porting any concept from a working artifact (team mapping, team labels, project tier, "former members", category→project routing), find the curated table in the source HTML/JS, copy it byte-for-byte into a `src/lib/<artifact>-<concept>.ts` file as a const Map or Set. (2) Normalize keys at load time (`name.trim().toLowerCase().replace(/\s+/g, ' ')`) so DevOps display-name drift (case, extra spaces) resolves correctly. (3) Wrap the lookup in a function that consults hardcoded FIRST, DB SECOND, fallback THIRD: `mapped = HARDCODED.get(norm) ?? db.get(norm) ?? FALLBACK`. (4) Add a code comment with the source line number so future updates can sync: `// v14:6231 ASSIGNEE_TO_TEAM — verbatim mirror`. (5) Apply same pattern across SBI, Action Log, Insights — any tab that resolves names. (6) Reference: May 5 — `src/lib/v14-team-mapping.ts:V14_ASSIGNEE_TO_TEAM` + `V14_FORMER_MEMBERS` + `src/components/TeamVelocity.tsx:teamCodeFor`.

649. **Electron `dev:all` does NOT recompile `electron/main.ts` between launches — Vite hot-reloads only the renderer, the main process loads from `dist-electron/electron/main.js` which is produced by a SEPARATE `tsc -p electron/tsconfig.json` step that no `npm run dev` invokes** — On 2026-05-05 swapping `seedTeamRosterIfEmpty` → `seedTeamRoster` in `electron/main.ts`. Restart cycle showed the OLD log line `[main] team_roster already populated; skipping seed` — proving the OLD compiled bundle was still loading. `package.json` `main: "dist-electron/electron/main.js"`. The `dev:all` script is `concurrently npm:dev npm:dev:electron` — neither runs `tsc`. Fix: ran `npm run build:electron` (which executes `tsc -p electron/tsconfig.json`) then restarted `dev:all`. Permanent fix: prepend `build:electron` to `dev:all` so the chain becomes `npm run build:electron && concurrently …`. **Why:** Electron's two-process architecture (main + renderer) has two separate build paths. Renderer = Vite + TS = HMR works. Main = standalone tsc compile to dist-electron, no watch in dev mode. Vite plugins like `vite-plugin-electron` paper over this but PMO_OS doesn't use one — pure separation. Documentation usually omits this because most Electron tutorials use a Vite plugin; when you don't, the two halves drift silently. **How to apply:** (1) When editing `electron/**/*.ts`, run `npm run build:electron` immediately after, BEFORE testing. (2) When the user reports "I restarted but the change didn't take", ALWAYS check whether the file lives in `electron/` (needs build:electron) vs `src/` (just hot-reloads). (3) Add `build:electron` to the front of the `dev:all` chain so this trap is impossible: `"dev:all": "npm run build:electron && concurrently …"`. (4) When the project supports cleanup (`Remove-Item dist-electron`), inform the user this DELETES the compiled bundle — they must run `build:electron` before `dev:all` will launch at all. (5) Reference: May 5 — `package.json:9` main field + `:12` dev:electron script; `dist-electron/electron/main.js:369` post-fix `seedTeamRoster()` call.

650. **`Microsoft.VSTS.Common.ClosedDate` is NOT a required Azure DevOps field — when filtering "delivered tasks", fall back to `Microsoft.VSTS.Common.StateChangeDate` for tasks in Closed/Done/Resolved state, OR you'll silently reject the majority of completed work and show 6× lower velocity than the working dashboard** — On 2026-05-05 PMO_OS Team Velocity showed 288 closed tasks vs v14's 1652 — a 5.7× gap that page-size and silent-error fixes didn't close (only +1 task gained from `$top=500` + retry). Inspecting v14:5534–5537 revealed: `var closedDate = f['Microsoft.VSTS.Common.ClosedDate'] || null; if (!closedDate && isClosed) { closedDate = f['Microsoft.VSTS.Common.StateChangeDate'] || null; }`. Many closed tasks in the user's DevOps org never had `ClosedDate` stamped (it's not auto-populated when state moves to Done — it's only set if a workflow rule or manual action writes it). PMO_OS rejected those tasks via `if (!task.closedDate) continue;` — the fix was extending `VELOCITY_DETAIL_FIELDS` to include `Microsoft.VSTS.Common.StateChangeDate` and applying the same fallback in `fetchVelocityTasksForProject`. **Why:** Azure DevOps work-item fields fall into "always present" (System.Id, System.State) vs "optional" (the Microsoft.VSTS.Common.* family). `ClosedDate` is in the optional family. v14 has been live for months and the fallback was discovered organically; without it PMO_OS is technically "more strict" but in practice "wrong." **How to apply:** (1) When porting any DevOps WIQL+batch fetch, treat every `Microsoft.VSTS.*` field as optional and design fallbacks BEFORE shipping. (2) For closedDate specifically, the chain is: `ClosedDate || (state ∈ closedSet ? StateChangeDate : null)`. (3) Always add a code comment with the v14 source line: `// L034 (v14 parity, ...) — v14:5534 fallback`. (4) When task counts diverge between two dashboards on the same DevOps org, ALWAYS check field-fallback completeness BEFORE assuming page-size or scope issues — the 6× gap was 100% explained by this one field. (5) Reference: May 5 — `src/lib/devops-fetch.ts` `VELOCITY_DETAIL_FIELDS` + `fetchVelocityTasksForProject` closedDate resolution.

651. **Azure DevOps `_apis/projects` defaults to `$top=100` and that limit is silently applied server-side — explicitly request `$top=500&stateFilter=wellFormed` matching the source artifact, otherwise orgs with > 100 projects lose work to the page boundary in a way that's invisible from the response** — On 2026-05-05 diagnosing PMO_OS Team Velocity 6× count gap. v14:4321 explicitly URL-encodes both params: `&$top=500&stateFilter=wellFormed`. PMO_OS `listProjects()` was hitting `_apis/projects?api-version=7.1` with no overrides — Azure default applies, returns first 100 projects in some unspecified order, response gives no signal that there were more. Fix: pass `$top=500&stateFilter=wellFormed` exactly as v14 does. Belt-and-braces: keep the client-side `state === 'wellFormed'` filter even though the server now applies it, in case the API version changes. **Why:** Default page sizes in REST APIs are silent traps because the response shape doesn't tell you "this is page 1 of 3" unless you look for a continuation token. Azure DevOps follows this pattern — clients that ignore $top look like they're getting "all" projects when they're really getting the first N. The user's test bed has > 100 projects, so the truncation matters; smaller orgs would never notice. **How to apply:** (1) Any time you call a DevOps `_apis/<collection>` endpoint, explicitly pass `$top=<bound>` matching the largest realistic count for the org. 500 is a safe ceiling for projects/teams/iterations. (2) For paginated results that might exceed the bound, implement continuation-token loops; for collections that are bounded by org structure (projects, teams), one-shot $top is fine. (3) Mirror the source artifact's exact query string when porting — easy to grep for `_apis/projects` in the v14 HTML and copy the params. (4) Add a code comment naming the source line: `// L033 (v14 parity, ...) — v14:4321 verbatim`. (5) Reference: May 5 — `src/lib/devops-fetch.ts:listProjects` URL with explicit `$top=500&stateFilter=wellFormed`.

652. **Per-project fetch errors in a multi-project loop must surface as a UI banner, not a silent `console.warn` — when the snapshot is missing tasks, the user has no way to know whether the data is incomplete or just smaller than expected unless the failures are visible** — On 2026-05-05 PMO_OS `runFetchWithPat()` had `try { await fetchVelocityTasksForProject(...); } catch (err) { console.warn(...); }` — every project that timed out, 401'd, or 404'd was silently dropped from the snapshot. User saw a smaller-than-expected count but no signal pointing at the cause. Fix: (a) added retry-once-with-500ms-backoff so transient errors recover, (b) accumulated final failures into `fetchErrors: { project, error }[]` array stored on the snapshot, (c) rendered a yellow `⚠ N projects failed to fetch (click to inspect)` `<details>` element on the tab with each project + error message + "retry the full fetch" hint. **Why:** Silent error handling optimizes for "the loop completes" — the wrong optimization. The user's mental model is "I clicked Refresh, I see the result." If 5 of 18 projects errored silently and the result looks small, the user wonders whether the data is wrong or whether they're misreading the dashboard. Visible errors collapse that ambiguity into "yes data is incomplete and here's why." **How to apply:** (1) Any loop iterating over external resources should accumulate errors in a structured array, never just log them. (2) Surface the error count + per-resource detail in the UI with a `<details>` block (cheap to ignore when empty, scannable when expanded). (3) Always retry once with backoff before recording a failure — most network errors resolve. (4) The error message must be the actual `err.message`, not a generic "fetch failed" — users need the underlying cause (timeout vs 401 vs 404 vs CORS) to triage. (5) Reference: May 5 — `src/components/TeamVelocity.tsx:runFetchWithPat` `fetchWithRetry` + `fetchErrors[]` + the ⚠ details UI block.

653. **Markdown-stored insights + a runtime markdown→HTML renderer (react-markdown + remark-gfm) gets you v14-style rich card visuals without any schema migration — the storage layer (text body) and the render layer (component overrides) are independent concerns and should be evolved independently** — On 2026-05-05 the Insights tab in PMO_OS rendered every saved insight body as `<pre>{section.body}</pre>` — markdown headings showed as `### Header` literal text, tables showed as pipe-character lines, bold/italic disappeared. v14 stores insights as structured JSON with kind-specific render functions (rich HTML tables, colored pills). Switching PMO_OS to v14's structured-JSON storage would have meant: schema change, data migration, per-kind render functions matching v14 exactly — ~2 days of work breaking all existing insights. Alternative chosen: keep markdown storage, install `react-markdown@^10.1.0` + `remark-gfm@^4.0.1`, replace `<pre>` with `<ReactMarkdown remarkPlugins={[remarkGfm]} components={MD_COMPONENTS}>`. `MD_COMPONENTS` is a 14-element record mapping `h1-h4`, `p`, `strong`, `em`, `ul/ol/li`, `code`, `pre`, `table/thead/th/td`, `a`, `blockquote`, `hr` → Tailwind-styled equivalents matching v14's small uppercase headings + bordered tables + monospace numerics. ~30 lines of override code, zero migration, every existing insight instantly renders rich. **Why:** Storage and render are different concerns. Markdown is a great storage format (human-readable, diffable, future-proof, easy to write programmatically via helper functions); the only thing it lacks is a renderer. Adding the renderer at the leaf component is cheaper than restructuring storage AND keeps storage format stable for next year's needs. The `remark-gfm` plugin specifically unlocks GitHub-flavored markdown tables — without it, react-markdown silently passes pipe-tables through as plain text. **How to apply:** (1) When stored content is markdown but rendering shows raw markdown source, the fix is a renderer at the component, not a storage migration. (2) Install `react-markdown` + `remark-gfm` together — gfm is required for tables, autolinks, strikethrough; just react-markdown alone is too minimal. (3) Define a single `MD_COMPONENTS` const at module scope so every insight card renders consistently. (4) Map each markdown element to a Tailwind-styled equivalent matching the source artifact's visual language: small uppercase `<h3>`, bordered `<table>` with `<thead>` accent bg, monospace `<td>`, inline code chips, etc. (5) Test by saving an insight that includes a pipe-table — confirm it renders as a real HTML table with borders, not pipe-character lines. (6) Reference: May 5 — `src/components/Insights.tsx:MD_COMPONENTS` + `package.json` deps `react-markdown@10.1.0` + `remark-gfm@4.0.1`.

654. **Auto-draft logic that lives in `lib/` is INVISIBLE to the user until a UI tab renders it — when the user dogfoods a tab and asks "where are the SLA signals?", the answer is to surface the existing helpers in the UI, not to build new logic** — On 2026-05-05 PMO_OS Outlook Categories tab showed only category inventory (counts, recent messages, flagged summary). v14's equivalent surface had Unreplied Emails (SLA > 48h) + Missed Mentions tables. PMO_OS already had `fetchEmailReplyTracking()` + `fetchMissedMentions()` helpers in `src/lib/outlook-server-fetch.ts` (port 5050 sidecar) AND `buildEmailReplyDrafts()` + `buildEmailMentionDrafts()` in `src/lib/auto-draft.ts` — all the SLA logic was wired, just not RENDERED. The user could see the auto-drafts in Action Log AFTER they ran but had no preview. Fix: added 2 sections to OutlookCategories.tsx with parallel `Promise.all` fetch + per-section loading/error/empty states + `emailZone()` color-coding mirroring auto-draft.ts thresholds. ~150 lines of UI, zero new helpers. **Why:** A library function that no UI calls is dead code from the user's perspective. PMO architecture treats `lib/` as the "engine" and components as the "dashboard"; both must exist for the engine to be usable. The trap is shipping the engine first (because it has tests, it's clean) and forgetting the dashboard never got the corresponding section. **How to apply:** (1) When dogfooding reveals a "where is X?" gap, GREP `src/lib/` for the concept — likely the helper exists already and the component just doesn't call it. (2) Map every `lib/*.ts` exported function to the component(s) that surface it; treat unmounted helpers as a documentation debt. (3) Surface auto-draft signals BEFORE drafts are committed — show the user "here's what would be drafted" so they can preempt false positives. (4) For multi-source tabs, fire all fetches in parallel via `Promise.all` so a slow sidecar doesn't block the fast one. (5) Each section gets its own loading/error/empty state so failures isolate. (6) Reference: May 5 — `src/components/OutlookCategories.tsx` Unreplied + Missed Mentions sections calling pre-existing `fetchEmailReplyTracking` + `fetchMissedMentions`.
